# PART I — GOVERNANCE FOUNDATION

## 1. PURPOSE

### 1.1 Why this manual exists

1. **Protect the people we serve and the people who work here.** [Organization Name] holds protected health information, resident support records, employee personnel data, donor records, and financial data. A single uncontained compromise can expose all of it at once.
2. **Keep services running.** Residential programs operate 24 hours a day, 365 days a year. Day programs, transportation, medication documentation, incident reporting, payroll, and Medicaid billing all now depend on network and cloud systems. Technology downtime is not an inconvenience in this organization — it is a service-delivery and regulatory event.
3. **Give staff one clear instruction set.** Most technology failures in organizations our size are not caused by sophisticated attackers. They are caused by unclear process: nobody knew who to call, nobody wrote it down, nobody closed the loop.
4. **Give leadership visibility.** Leadership cannot manage risk it cannot see. This manual defines the measurements, the reporting cadence, and the escalation thresholds that convert scattered technology activity into a reviewable management picture.
5. **Create mechanisms, not one-time fixes.** Every recurring problem addressed in this manual is assigned an owner, an instrument (a number reviewed on a cadence), and an escalation threshold. A procedure that depends on one person's memory is a liability, not a control.

### 1.2 What this manual is not

1. This manual is **not** a technical build guide. It does not contain system configurations, network diagrams, administrator credentials, or security-control settings. Those live in the restricted technical documentation maintained by [Director of Information Technology] and [IT Provider Name].
2. This manual is **not** a substitute for the Employee Handbook, the HIPAA Privacy and Security Policies, the Corporate Compliance Plan, or the Incident Reporting (UIR) procedures. Where this manual and those documents conflict, ⬜ **[INSERT WHICH DOCUMENT CONTROLS — recommend: the more restrictive requirement controls, and the conflict is reported to [Chief Operating Officer] and [VP Compliance] within 5 business days]**.
3. This manual does **not** create a contract of employment or alter any employee's at-will status.

---

## 2. SCOPE

### 2.1 Who this manual covers

Applies to every person who accesses [Organization Name] systems, data, networks, devices, or accounts, including:

| Category | Covered | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Full-time and part-time employees | Yes | All departments, all sites |
| Direct Support Professionals and program staff | Yes | Including shared-workstation and kiosk users |
| Supervisors, managers, directors, executives | Yes | Plus supervisory duties in Section 5 |
| Per-diem, temporary, and seasonal staff | Yes | Access time-boxed; see SOP-09 |
| Interns and students | Yes | Sponsor supervisor required |
| Volunteers | Yes | Only if granted an account or device |
| Independent contractors and consultants | Yes | Contract must reference this manual |
| Vendors with system or network access | Yes | Including MSP, EHR, billing, HVAC/BAS, and fleet telematics vendors |
| Board members | Yes | For organizational email, board portal, and file access |
| Family members and guardians | No | Communications with them are governed by SOP-01 and HIPAA policy |
| Individuals receiving services | No | Assistive and resident-facing technology is covered under SOP-24 and program policy |

### 2.2 What this manual covers

1. All organization-owned or organization-leased computing devices: desktops, laptops, tablets, mobile phones, shared program workstations, kiosks, time clocks, and printers/multifunction devices.
2. All personally owned devices used to access organizational email, files, or systems (see SOP-12).
3. All networks: office wired and wireless, residential-site networks, guest Wi-Fi, cellular hotspots, and remote access.
4. All cloud and hosted applications, whether centrally procured or departmentally procured.
5. All organizational data in any form, including paper records generated from or printed by organizational systems.
6. All telephone and voice services, including desk phones, VoIP softphones, call groups, voicemail, and organization-issued cell phones.
7. All artificial intelligence tools used for organizational work, whether paid, free, embedded in an approved product, or accessed personally (see SOP-10).
8. All physical locations: administrative offices, group homes, supervised apartments, adult day program sites, the ICF, maintenance and fleet facilities, and remote/telework locations.

## 3. GUIDING PRINCIPLES

| # | Principle | What it means in practice |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Resident and employee data comes first** | When a control is inconvenient and protective, it stays. Convenience is never a sufficient reason for an exception. |
| 2 | **Services must not stop** | Every control is designed with a documented fallback so that a security measure never becomes the cause of a service interruption in a home or program. |
| 3 | **One front door** | Staff should never have to decide who to contact. One work-order link, one support number, one emergency path. |
| 4 | **Instrument before intervening** | If leadership cannot see a number weekly, leadership cannot manage it. Every control in this manual produces a measurement. |
| 5 | **One source of truth per data domain** | One asset inventory. One ticket system. One site roster. One vendor list. Parallel spreadsheets are treated as a defect to be corrected, not a workaround to be tolerated. |
| 6 | **Least privilege by default** | People get the minimum access needed for the job they hold today. Access is granted by role, reviewed quarterly, and removed on departure. |
| 7 | **Automate the transaction, keep the judgment** | Routing, reminders, aggregation, and logging are automated. Decisions about people, money, services, and risk stay with accountable humans. |
| 8 | **Build inside governance, never around it** | Any capability that cannot survive a HIPAA, funder, or audit review is not a capability. |
| 9 | **Designed for handoff** | Every procedure names an owner and a backup. Nothing in this manual may depend on a single named individual being reachable. |
| 10 | **Cost-conscious by design** | Controls are chosen for effectiveness per dollar. Where a lower-cost control achieves the outcome, it is preferred and the reasoning is documented. |
| 11 | **Report, don't hide** | No employee is ever disciplined for promptly and honestly reporting a security mistake they made. Concealment is the disciplinary event. |
| 12 | **Accessibility is a requirement, not a feature** | Technology used by staff and stakeholders with disabilities must be usable by them. See SOP-24. |

## 4. DEFINITIONS AND ABBREVIATIONS

### 4.1 Organizational and regulatory terms

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **BAA** | Business Associate Agreement. A HIPAA-required contract with any vendor that creates, receives, maintains, or transmits PHI on our behalf. |
| **CMS** | Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services. Federal regulator of the ICF under 42 CFR Part 483. |
| **CoP** | Conditions of Participation. Federal requirements an ICF/IID must meet to remain certified. |
| **DDD** | New Jersey Division of Developmental Disabilities. Licenses and funds community program and residential services. |
| **DSP** | Direct Support Professional. |
| **EVV** | Electronic Visit Verification. |
| **FFS** | Fee-for-service. The Medicaid payment model on which the majority of organizational revenue depends. |
| **ICF/IID** | Intermediate Care Facility for Individuals with Intellectual Disabilities. Governed by CMS under 42 CFR Part 483 and surveyed by the New Jersey Department of Health. |
| **N.J.A.C. 10:44A / 10:44C** | New Jersey administrative code chapters governing community residential and day program services. |
| **PHI** | Protected Health Information. Individually identifiable health information covered by HIPAA. |
| **PII** | Personally Identifiable Information. Information that identifies a specific person (name, SSN, DOB, address, account numbers, biometric data). |
| **QIDP** | Qualified Intellectual Disabilities Professional. |
| **UIR** | Unusual Incident Report. Required program incident reporting to DDD. **A cybersecurity incident is not a substitute for a UIR and a UIR is not a substitute for a cybersecurity incident report; where an event affects both, both are filed.** |

### 4.2 Technology terms (plain language)

| Term | Plain-language definition |
|---|---|
| **Account lockout** | Your account is temporarily blocked, usually after too many wrong password attempts. |
| **Approved AI tool** | An AI product on the [Approved AI Tool List] that has been reviewed and contracted by the organization. |
| **Automated decision-making** | A system deciding an outcome about a person without a human reviewing it. |
| **Confidential information** | Any information that would harm a person or the organization if disclosed. See SOP-14 for the full classification. |
| **Encryption** | Scrambling data so it is unreadable without a key. Protects data on a lost laptop or phone. |
| **Generative AI** | Software that produces new text, images, audio, code, or summaries from a prompt. |
| **Human review** | A qualified person reads AI output, verifies it, and takes responsibility for it before it is used. |
| **High-risk use** | Any use of technology or AI that affects a person's services, housing, benefits, employment, health, money, or legal rights. |
| **Least privilege** | You get access to what your job requires — nothing more. |
| **MFA / 2-Step Verification** | A second proof of identity beyond your password (app prompt, code, or security key). |
| **Managed Service Provider (MSP)** | The outside IT company contracted to operate and support our technology: [IT Provider Name]. |
| **Multifunction device (MFD)** | A combined printer, copier, scanner, and fax machine. |
| **Phishing** | A fake message designed to trick you into clicking, logging in, paying, or sharing information. |
| **Privileged account** | An account with administrative power over systems, data, or other accounts. |
| **Public AI tool** | Any AI service not on the [Approved AI Tool List] — including free consumer versions and personal accounts. |
| **Ransomware** | Malicious software that locks or encrypts files and demands payment. |
| **Shadow IT** | Any application, cloud service, or device used for work that was never approved or inventoried. |
| **Shared Drive** | A Google Workspace shared team folder owned by the organization, not by an individual. |
| **Social engineering** | Manipulating a person — by phone, email, text, or in person — into breaking a security rule. |
| **VoIP** | Voice over Internet Protocol. Telephone service delivered over the internet. |
| **Work order** | A logged request for IT, Facilities, or Fleet help submitted through [Work Order Submission Link]. |

## 5. ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES

### 5.1 Every employee, contractor, intern, volunteer, and Board member

1. Complete assigned security, privacy, and AI training on schedule.
2. Use organizational accounts for organizational work.
3. Protect your password and never share it with anyone, including IT, supervisors, or executives.
4. Report suspected phishing, malware, lost devices, and privacy incidents **immediately** — within one hour of noticing.
5. Submit work orders through [Work Order Submission Link] rather than informal channels.
6. Use only approved software, cloud services, and AI tools.
7. Handle confidential information according to SOP-14.
8. Return all organizational devices, keys, badges, and records on separation or upon request.
9. Sign the Annual Staff Acknowledgement (Appendix J).

### 5.2 Supervisors, managers, and site/program leads

1. Ensure your team completes required training; monitor your team's completion report.
2. Submit access requests (Appendix B) **before** a new employee's start date — not on the start date.
3. Submit offboarding notice (Appendix D) to [Human Resources Contact] and IT as soon as a separation is known.
4. Approve, in writing, what access each team member needs; you are accountable for over-provisioned access on your team.
5. Verify your team members are submitting work orders rather than working around broken technology.
6. Maintain awareness of which devices and assets are physically located at your site (SOP-11).
7. Escalate per the criteria in each SOP; do not absorb a problem that meets an escalation threshold.
8. Certify your team's access during the quarterly access review (SOP-09).
9. Collect organizational property at separation and confirm receipt on the offboarding form.

### 5.3 [Director of Information Technology]

1. Own day-to-day technology operations, the MSP relationship, and the technical accuracy of this manual.
2. Maintain the asset inventory, access records, incident log, change log, and vendor register.
3. Triage, assign, and track all work orders to closure within service-level targets.
4. Produce the monthly executive technology dashboard (Part IV).
5. Lead technical response to security incidents and coordinate with the MSP and cyber carrier.
6. Maintain the [Approved AI Tool List] and the approved software register.
7. Execute quarterly access reviews and privileged-account reviews.
8. Verify backup testing and restoration testing on the defined cadence.
9. Escalate to [Chief Operating Officer] at the thresholds defined in each SOP.

### 5.4 [IT Provider Name] — Managed Service Provider

1. Deliver support, monitoring, patching, backup, and incident response per the executed service agreement.
2. Meet the contracted service-level targets (SOP-16).
3. Perform privileged administrative work and document it in the ticket record.
4. Provide a named account manager, a documented escalation path, and 24/7 emergency contact.
5. Provide monthly performance reporting in the format required by SOP-16.
6. Never perform a password reset or access change without a request that meets SOP-02 and SOP-09 verification standards.
7. Notify [Director of Information Technology] of any suspected security incident within ⬜ **[INSERT — recommend 30 minutes]** of detection.

### 5.5 [Chief Operating Officer]

1. Own this manual and the overall technology operating model.
2. Approve service-level targets, the technology budget, major platform decisions, and exceptions above the delegated threshold.
3. Chair the Technology Governance Committee (Section 11.4).
4. Review the executive dashboard monthly and report technology risk to the CEO and Board.
5. Declare and stand down major incidents (SOP-17).
6. Ensure cross-functional cooperation where this manual depends on peer departments (Section 6).

### 5.6 [Director of Finance]

1. Approve technology spend within the approved budget and enforce the procurement path in SOP-13.
2. Own the payment-fraud verification controls in SOP-08.6 and Appendix H.
3. Ensure no vendor banking change is executed without out-of-band verification.
4. Ensure business continuity for payroll and Medicaid billing during outages (SOP-25).
5. Maintain cyber-liability coverage in coordination with the insurance broker and confirm the organization meets policy conditions.

### 5.7 Peer departments — cooperation dependencies

| Department | Dependency this manual creates | Owner |
|---|---|---|
| **Human Resources** | Timely start/change/separation notice; monitoring notice in handbook; discipline administration; acknowledgement collection | [Chief Human Resources Officer] |
| **Compliance / Quality** | HIPAA breach risk assessment and determination; regulatory notification decisions; audit and survey readiness | [VP Compliance, Quality & Special Projects] |
| **Clinical / Nursing** | Clinical system access definitions; PHI handling in clinical workflows; downtime clinical documentation procedures | [Chief Clinical Officer] / [VP Nursing] |
| **Residential & Day Program Operations** | Site-level execution of outage procedures; device custody at sites; staff training completion | [VP of Operations] |
| **Development / Marketing** | Donor data handling; website and social media governance (SOP-23) | [Director of Development] |

## 6. AUTHORITY AND OWNERSHIP BOUNDARIES

> **Read this section before implementation. It is the difference between a plan that executes and a plan that stalls.**

### 6.1 Direct authority

The following are within the [Chief Operating Officer]'s direct line and can be implemented by management decision: Information Technology, Facilities and Fleet, Procurement and vendor management, Finance operations, Business Development, and the operational infrastructure of this manual (work orders, assets, networks, telephony, MSP performance, technology budget and lifecycle).

### 6.2 Shared or peer authority — requires agreement, not instruction

| Manual element | Peer function that must agree | Why | If agreement is not obtained |
|---|---|---|---|
| Enforcement and discipline (Section 10) | Human Resources | Discipline is administered by HR under the handbook | The manual has no teeth; controls become advisory |
| Monitoring and no-expectation-of-privacy notice (SOP-07.6) | HR + Legal | Must appear in handbook and acknowledgement to be enforceable | Monitoring may be legally challengeable |
| HIPAA breach determination and notification (SOP-08) | Compliance | Breach risk assessment authority sits with Compliance/Privacy Officer | Risk of missed or late regulatory notification |
| Clinical system access roles (SOP-09) | Clinical / Nursing | Only clinical leadership can define clinically appropriate access | Access matrix will be wrong and will fail audit |
| AI restrictions on clinical and disciplinary content (SOP-10) | Clinical + HR + Legal | Those decisions are not the COO's to constrain alone | Policy unenforceable in the functions it most affects |
| Training mandate and completion tracking (Section 9) | HR | Training assignment and completion records live in the HR system | Completion KPI cannot be produced |
| Records retention schedule (SOP-21) | Compliance + Legal | Retention periods are legal determinations | Retention periods in this manual remain proposals |

### 6.3 How to handle the boundary

1. **Do not present peer-function requirements as directives.** Present them as the technology-side dependency and ask the peer executive to co-own the section.
2. **Seek explicit co-signature** from HR, Compliance, and Clinical on the approval page. Co-signature converts a cross-functional requirement from an imposition into a joint standard.
3. **Where a peer function declines to adopt a provision,** record it in the Exception Register (Section 7) as an open governance gap rather than publishing an unenforceable rule.
4. **Adoption note for leadership:** this manual touches nearly every department. Introduced as an IT mandate from a newly created executive office, it will read to some leaders as centralization. Introduced as a set of shared controls with visible peer co-ownership and a demonstrable staff benefit (faster support, fewer outages, one front door), it reads as service. Sequence the rollout accordingly — see Part VII, Phase 1.

---

## 7. POLICY EXCEPTIONS PROCESS

### 7.1 Principle

An exception is a documented, time-limited, risk-accepted deviation. An undocumented deviation is a violation.

### 7.2 Procedure

1. **Identify** the specific requirement that cannot be met and the operational reason.
2. **Complete** the Technology Exception Request Form (Appendix I).
3. **Propose** a compensating control — an alternative safeguard that reduces the risk the original control addressed.
4. **Submit** to [Director of Information Technology] for technical risk assessment.
5. **Route** for approval per the authority table below.
6. **If approved, then** IT records the exception in the Exception Register with an expiration date not to exceed 12 months.
7. **If denied, then** the requester is notified in writing with the reason, and the original requirement stands.
8. **Review** all open exceptions quarterly at the Technology Governance Committee.
9. **Expire or renew** each exception at its end date. An expired exception is automatically closed and the requirement is enforced.

### 7.3 Approval authority

| Risk level | Examples | Approver | Maximum duration |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Low** | Non-standard peripheral; minor software variance with no data access | [Director of Information Technology] | 12 months |
| **Moderate** | Delayed device replacement; local admin rights for a specific business application; non-standard file-sharing method | [Chief Operating Officer] | 6 months |
| **High** | Any deviation affecting MFA, encryption, backups, PHI handling, payment controls, or offboarding timing | [Chief Operating Officer] + [VP Compliance] | 90 days |
| **Critical** | Anything that would place the organization out of compliance with HIPAA, CMS CoP, DDD licensing, or cyber-insurance policy conditions | [Executive Director/CEO] + [Legal Counsel Contact] + insurance carrier notification | 30 days, renewable once |

### 7.4 Never eligible for exception

1. Sharing passwords between individuals.
2. Disabling MFA on an account with access to PHI, finance, payroll, HR, or administrative privileges.
3. Resetting a password based solely on an emailed request (SOP-02).
4. Executing a vendor banking change without out-of-band verification (SOP-08.6).
5. Entering PHI, resident identifiers, or employee personnel data into a public AI tool (SOP-10).
6. Using an unencrypted device to store confidential information.
7. Bypassing offboarding for a separation involving a workplace conduct concern.

## 8. COMPLIANCE, CONFIDENTIALITY, AND RECORDS RETENTION STATEMENT

### 8.1 Regulatory framework

This manual is designed to support compliance with, at minimum: HIPAA Privacy, Security, and Breach Notification Rules (45 CFR Parts 160 and 164); 42 CFR Part 483 Subpart I (ICF/IID Conditions of Participation); N.J.A.C. 10:44A and 10:44C; the New Jersey Identity Theft Prevention Act breach-notification requirement (N.J.S.A. 56:8-163); applicable Medicaid program integrity and documentation requirements; and the organization's grant, contract, donor, and insurance obligations.

⬜ **[LEGAL REVIEW REQUIRED — Legal Counsel must confirm this list is complete and current for [Organization Name] before adoption.]**

### 8.2 Confidentiality

1. All organizational information is confidential unless expressly designated public.
2. Access to information is granted for the performance of assigned duties only.
3. Confidentiality obligations survive the end of employment or contract indefinitely.
4. Accessing records of individuals, employees, or donors without a job-related need is prohibited and is independently a HIPAA violation where PHI is involved.

### 8.3 Records retention — summary

Full schedule in SOP-21. Retention periods below are ⬜ **proposed and require Legal and Compliance confirmation**.

| Record type | Proposed retention | System of record |
|---|---|---|
| Email (general business) | 7 years | Google Workspace + Google Vault |
| Email subject to legal hold | Until hold released | Google Vault |
| Work orders and ticket history | 7 years | [Work Order System] |
| Security incident records | 7 years minimum; HIPAA-related 6 years from resolution | Incident log |
| Access request / change / removal records | 7 years after separation | HR system + IT access log |
| Asset assignment and disposal records | 7 years after disposal | Asset inventory |
| Backup and restore test records | 3 years | MSP reporting |
| Vendor contracts and BAAs | 7 years after termination | Contract repository |
| AI tool approvals and risk assessments | Life of tool + 7 years | AI register |
| Training and acknowledgement records | 7 years after separation | HR system |
| Board and governance materials | Permanent | Board portal |

### 8.4 Legal hold

1. When litigation, investigation, audit, or survey is reasonably anticipated, [Legal Counsel Contact] or [VP Compliance] issues a written legal hold.
2. Upon receipt, all deletion of covered records **stops immediately**, including routine auto-deletion.
3. IT applies a technical hold in Google Vault and confirms application in writing within ⬜ **[recommend 1 business day]**.
4. Holds are released only in writing by the issuer. See SOP-21.

---

## 9. TRAINING AND ACKNOWLEDGEMENT REQUIREMENTS

### 9.1 Required training

| Training | Audience | Timing | Frequency | Owner |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| IT & Cybersecurity Orientation | All new users | Before account activation | Once | IT + HR |
| Annual Security Awareness | All users | Within 30 days of assignment | Annual | IT |
| Phishing simulation | All email users | Ongoing | ⬜ [recommend monthly or quarterly] | IT / MSP |
| HIPAA Privacy & Security | All users with PHI access | Before PHI access | Annual | Compliance |
| AI Use Standard | All users with AI tool access | Before AI tool access | Annual | IT + COO |
| Payment Fraud Verification | Finance, AP, procurement, executives, anyone who can request or approve a payment | Before payment authority | Annual + after any BEC attempt | Finance |
| Work Order & Support Process | All staff; refresher for supervisors | Within 30 days of hire | Annual | IT |
| Incident Response Role Training | Incident response team members | On assignment | Annual + after each tabletop | IT + COO |
| Site Outage Procedures | Site and program leadership | Within 30 days of role start | Annual | IT + Operations |

### 9.2 Acknowledgement

1. Every covered person signs the Annual Staff Acknowledgement (Appendix J) at onboarding and annually thereafter.
2. Acknowledgement is collected and retained by ⬜ **[INSERT — HR system or IT system; recommend HR system as the single source of truth]**.
3. **If an employee does not complete required training within 30 days of assignment, then** IT notifies the supervisor; **if not completed within 45 days, then** the supervisor's director is notified; **if not completed within 60 days, then** [Chief Operating Officer] and [Chief Human Resources Officer] determine whether access is suspended.
4. Access suspension for training noncompliance requires ⬜ **[HR AND LEGAL REVIEW — confirm this is consistent with the Employee Handbook and any collective bargaining obligations before adoption]**.

---

## 10. ENFORCEMENT AND CONSEQUENCES FOR NONCOMPLIANCE

⬜ **[HR AND LEGAL REVIEW REQUIRED FOR THIS ENTIRE SECTION BEFORE ADOPTION.]**

### 10.1 Principle

1. The organization distinguishes between **mistakes** (which are expected, and must be reported), **negligence** (repeated disregard of a known requirement), and **willful misconduct** (deliberate violation, concealment, or misuse).
2. **Prompt self-reporting of a security mistake will not itself result in discipline.** Failure to report, or concealment, is a separate and more serious matter.

### 10.2 Graduated response

| Conduct | Typical response |
|---|---|
| Good-faith error, promptly reported | Coaching, refresher training. No discipline. |
| Repeated procedural noncompliance after coaching | Documented counseling, supervisor follow-up, possible access restriction |
| Unauthorized access to records without job-related need | Investigation by HR and Compliance; potential termination; HIPAA reporting obligations |
| Password sharing or MFA circumvention | Immediate access review; documented discipline |
| Concealing a security incident | Serious disciplinary action up to termination |
| Deliberate data theft, fraud, or sabotage | Termination; referral to law enforcement; civil recovery |
| Use of AI in a prohibited high-risk decision | Investigation; discipline proportionate to harm; decision reversed and re-made with human process |

### 10.3 Non-employee noncompliance

1. **Contractors and vendors:** access suspended immediately; contract remedies pursued by [Director of Finance] and [Legal Counsel Contact].
2. **Volunteers and interns:** access removed; sponsoring supervisor notified.
3. **Board members:** referred to [Board Chair] under the Board's own governance and conflict policies.

---

## 11. ANNUAL REVIEW, TESTING, AND UPDATE PROCESS

### 11.1 Annual review

1. **Schedule** the review to conclude 60 days before the fiscal year begins so that budget implications flow into the technology budget (SOP-22).
2. **Collect** the prior year's incident log, exception register, dashboard trend data, MSP performance reports, audit and survey findings, and cyber-insurance requirements.
3. **Assign** each SOP to its named owner for redline.
4. **Review** redlines at the Technology Governance Committee.
5. **Route** changed sections affecting HR, Compliance, Clinical, Legal, or Finance to those executives for re-approval.
6. **Publish** the new version, update the revision history, and reissue the acknowledgement.
7. **Retire** the prior version to the archive; do not delete it (SOP-21).

### 11.2 Required annual testing

| Test | Scope | Frequency | Owner | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Backup restoration test | Sample restore from each critical system | ⬜ [recommend quarterly] | IT + MSP | Restore log with timestamps |
| Disaster recovery exercise | Simulated loss of a primary system | Annual | IT + COO | Exercise report |
| Tabletop incident exercise | Ransomware and BEC scenarios | Annual | COO + IT + Compliance + Finance | After-action report |
| Site outage drill | One site, all continuity steps | ⬜ [recommend semi-annual, rotating sites] | Operations + IT | Drill checklist |
| Access review | All users, all systems | Quarterly | IT + supervisors | Signed certification |
| Privileged account review | All admin accounts | Quarterly | IT + COO | Signed certification |
| Phishing simulation campaign | All email users | ⬜ [recommend monthly/quarterly] | IT / MSP | Campaign report |
| Vendor / MSP performance review | All Tier 1 and Tier 2 vendors | Quarterly | IT + Finance | Scorecard |
| Asset physical verification | All sites, rotating | Annual (each site once) | IT + site leads | Reconciliation report |

### 11.3 Triggered (off-cycle) review

Conduct an immediate targeted review when any of the following occur: a Severity 1 or Severity 2 incident; a confirmed breach; a change of core platform, EHR, MSP, or ISP; a regulatory change; an audit or survey finding; a change in cyber-insurance conditions; or an acquisition, merger, or new program site.

### 11.4 Technology Governance Committee

1. **Chair:** [Chief Operating Officer].
2. **Members:** Director of IT, Director of Finance, VP of Operations, Director of Facilities & Fleet, CHRO or designee, VP Compliance or designee, Clinical designee, MSP account manager (for the operational portion of the agenda only).
3. **Cadence:** ⬜ **[recommend monthly for the first 6 months, then quarterly]**.
4. **Standing agenda:** dashboard review; open Severity 1–2 incidents; exception register; access review status; vendor performance; AI tool register; budget and lifecycle; policy changes.
5. **Output:** decisions logged with owner and date; unresolved items carried with an aging count.

---
# PART II — CORE STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES

> **Standard SOP structure.** Every SOP in Parts II and III uses the same fifteen-part structure: A. Title · B. Number · C. Purpose · D. Scope · E. Owner · F. Required Systems, Tools, Forms, or Links · G. Definitions · H. Roles and Responsibilities · I. Procedure · J. Escalation Criteria · K. Required Documentation and Record Retention · L. Service-Level Target · M. Quality-Control Checklist · N. Related Policies and Forms · O. Revision Notes.

---

# SOP-01 — EMAIL USAGE AND MANAGEMENT

**B. SOP Number:** ITG-SOP-01 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

## C. Purpose
1. Establish how organizational email is used, protected, retained, and monitored.
2. Prevent the disclosure of resident, employee, donor, and financial information through email.
3. Ensure email remains a reliable, professional, and legally defensible business record.
4. Reduce the organization's exposure to phishing and business email compromise, which are the most common and most expensive attacks against nonprofits of our size.

## D. Scope
Applies to all organizational email accounts, distribution groups, shared mailboxes, aliases, and calendar/contacts data in Google Workspace, accessed from any device or location.

## E. Owner
**Accountable:** [Chief Operating Officer] · **Responsible:** [Director of Information Technology] · **Consulted:** HR, Compliance, Legal, Marketing/Communications

## F. Required Systems, Tools, Forms, or Links
| Item | Reference |
|---|---|
| Email platform | Google Workspace (Gmail) |
| Archive / eDiscovery | Google Vault |
| Secure file sharing | [Approved File-Sharing Platform] — Google Drive Shared Drives |
| Phishing report method | Gmail **Report phishing** + forward to ⬜ [INSERT PHISHING REPORT ADDRESS, e.g. phishing@[domain]] |
| Support | [Work Order Submission Link] · [IT Support Email] · [IT Support Phone] |
| Forms | Appendix K (Signature Standard), Appendix L (Email Templates) |

## G. Definitions
| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Organizational email** | An @[domain] account issued by [Organization Name]. |
| **External sender** | Any message originating outside @[domain]. Gmail applies an external banner. |
| **Shared mailbox / delegated mailbox** | A mailbox multiple staff access under their own credentials, e.g. intake@, billing@. |
| **Records preservation / legal hold** | A directive stopping deletion of messages (Section 8.4, SOP-21). |
| **Sensitive attachment** | Any file containing PHI, PII, financial account data, personnel data, or donor data. |

## H. Roles and Responsibilities
| Role | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| **Employee** | Use organizational email for organizational work; follow the Before You Send checklist; report phishing within 1 hour; set out-of-office for absences over one business day |
| **Supervisor** | Model standards; ensure coverage during absences; request shared mailboxes rather than sharing individual credentials; approve delegation requests |
| **IT** | Provision and deprovision accounts; maintain filtering, external-sender banners, and DMARC/SPF/DKIM; configure Vault retention; execute preservation orders |
| **Compliance** | Determine whether an email disclosure constitutes a reportable HIPAA breach |
| **Legal** | Issue and release legal holds; direct responses to subpoenas and records requests |
| **Marketing/Communications** | Own mass email, donor email, and media responses (SOP-23) |

## I. Procedure

### I.1 — Organizational email versus personal email
1. **Use** your organizational account for all work communication — internal, resident-related, vendor, donor, funder, regulator, and Board.
2. **Do not** conduct organizational business from a personal email account (Gmail personal, Yahoo, Hotmail, AOL, or a prior employer's address).
3. **Do not** send organizational information to your personal email account for any reason, including convenience, printing at home, or record-keeping.
4. **If** you receive organizational business at your personal account (for example, a vendor has the wrong address), **then**:
   a. Forward the message once to your organizational account.
   b. Reply from your organizational account.
   c. Ask the sender to correct their records.
   d. Delete the copy from your personal account.
   e. **If** the message contained PHI or PII, **then** report it to [IT Support Email] and Compliance the same day.
5. **Do not** use your organizational email to register for personal services, shopping, or social media.
6. **Do not** auto-forward organizational email to any external address (see I.9).

### I.2 — Checking email, response times, and coverage
1. **Check** email at the frequency assigned to your role:

| Role group | Minimum check frequency | Target response time (business hours) |
|---|---|---|
| Executives, directors, managers | Twice daily minimum | Same business day; 4 hours for internal urgent |
| Administrative, finance, billing, HR support | Twice daily minimum | 1 business day |
| Program/site supervisors and coordinators | Once per shift or daily | 1 business day |
| DSPs and staff on shared workstations | Per supervisor's schedule; at least once per assigned shift where account is issued | 2 business days |
| ⬜ [Add role groups] | ⬜ | ⬜ |

2. **Acknowledge** a message you cannot fully answer within the target window with a brief holding reply stating when you will respond.
3. **Do not** treat email as an emergency channel. **If** a matter is urgent or life-safety, **then** call — see I.6 and SOP-03.
4. **Set** an out-of-office reply for any absence exceeding one business day (template in I.12).
5. **Arrange** coverage before planned absences: **if** your absence exceeds five business days, **then** request mailbox delegation through [Work Order Submission Link] with supervisor approval. **Never** give another person your password to provide coverage.

### I.3 — Professional writing standards
1. **Write** subject lines that state the topic and the action needed. Use this format:
   `[Category] – Topic – Action Needed by Date`
   - `ACTION – FY26 Facilities Budget Worksheet – Return by 10/3`
   - `FYI – Fleet Inspection Schedule – No Action`
   - `DECISION NEEDED – Bergen Site Internet Circuit Upgrade – By 10/10`
   - `URGENT – [Site ID] Phone System Down – Immediate`
2. **State** the ask in the first two sentences. Put background below it.
3. **Use** short paragraphs, numbered lists, and bold for deadlines.
4. **Write** as if the message will be read aloud in a deposition, a survey exit conference, or a Board meeting — because it may be.
5. **Do not** use email for disciplinary conversations, performance criticism, or emotionally charged disputes. Speak in person or by phone and document the outcome factually afterward.
6. **Do not** use all capitals, sarcasm, humor about individuals served, or informal shorthand in messages touching resident care, HR, or legal matters.
7. **Proofread** before sending. **If** the message is going to a funder, regulator, donor, the Board, or the media, **then** have a second person review it.

### I.4 — Reply, Reply All, CC, and BCC
1. **Use Reply** by default.
2. **Use Reply All** only when every recipient needs the information to act. **If** you are only saying "thanks" or "agreed," **then** reply to the sender alone.
3. **Use CC** for people who need awareness but no action. Name the reason in the body: "Copying [Name] for visibility; no action needed."
4. **Use BCC** only to protect recipient privacy in a one-way announcement to a group who should not see each other's addresses (for example, a message to multiple families or donors).
5. **Do not** use BCC to secretly copy a supervisor, HR, or Legal into a conversation. **If** someone needs to be aware, **then** forward the message separately after sending, or CC openly.
6. **Do not** add recipients to a thread that contains PHI, personnel information, or legal discussion without confirming each new recipient has a job-related need to know. **If** unsure, **then** start a new message instead of forwarding the thread.
7. **Remove** recipients who no longer need the thread, and say so: "Dropping [Name] — item resolved."

### I.5 — Protecting confidential and sensitive information
1. **Classify** before you type. See SOP-14.
2. **Do not** include the following in the subject line, ever: an individual's name plus a diagnosis or service detail; a Social Security number; an account number; a password.
3. **Minimize**: include the least information needed. Use a record identifier rather than a name where the recipient can resolve it.
4. **Verify** the recipient. Gmail autocomplete is the leading cause of misdirected PHI. **Check** every address before sending, especially where two contacts have similar names.
5. **If** the message contains PHI or PII and the recipient is external, **then** do **not** attach it. Share a link from [Approved File-Sharing Platform] restricted to the named recipient, or use the approved secure-send method ⬜ **[INSERT — e.g. Google Workspace client-side encryption, Gmail confidential mode, or approved secure portal; confirm the chosen method with Compliance and the MSP]**.
6. **Do not** use Gmail confidential mode as a substitute for a Business Associate Agreement or as proof of HIPAA compliance. ⬜ **[COMPLIANCE REVIEW REQUIRED on acceptable transmission methods for PHI.]**
7. **Do not** forward resident information to personal devices, personal accounts, or unapproved collaboration tools.
8. **If** you send an email to the wrong recipient, **then** follow I.5.9 immediately — do not attempt to quietly fix it.
9. **Misdirected email procedure:**
   a. Do not delete the sent message — it is evidence.
   b. Notify your supervisor immediately.
   c. Report to [IT Support Email] and Compliance within **1 hour**.
   d. If possible, contact the unintended recipient and request deletion; document the request and response.
   e. Complete the Cybersecurity/Privacy Incident Report (Appendix E).
   f. Compliance determines whether a HIPAA breach risk assessment and notification are required. **This determination is not made by IT or by the sender.**

### I.6 — External communications and special categories

| Situation | Do this | Do not do this |
|---|---|---|
| **Media inquiry** | Forward immediately, unanswered, to [Marketing/Communications Contact] and [Executive Director/CEO]. Reply to the reporter only: "Thank you — I am routing your request to our communications office, who will respond." | Never confirm, deny, or comment — including "no comment" with detail |
| **Legal matter, subpoena, records request, attorney letter** | Forward to [Legal Counsel Contact] and [VP Compliance] the same day. Preserve everything. | Never reply substantively, never delete anything, never forward to colleagues for discussion |
| **Regulatory / licensing (DDD, DOH, CMS, Medicaid)** | Forward to [VP Compliance] and [Chief Operating Officer] immediately | Never answer on your own |
| **Resident/family complaint or guardian request** | Route per program policy to the responsible program leader and Compliance | Never discuss a resident's information with anyone whose authority you have not verified |
| **Vendor pricing, scope, or contract terms** | Route to [Director of Finance] and the contracting owner | Never commit the organization to spend or scope by email without approval authority |
| **Any payment, banking, wire, ACH, or invoice change request** | Stop. Execute SOP-08.6 verification. | Never act on emailed payment instructions without out-of-band verbal verification |
| **Donor or grant communication** | Coordinate with [Director of Development] | Never make representations about restricted fund use without Development and Finance |
| **Employment reference request** | Forward to [Human Resources Contact] | Never provide a reference personally |

### I.7 — Attachments and secure file sharing
1. **Prefer a link over an attachment.** Links can be revoked; attachments cannot.
2. **Share** files from [Approved File-Sharing Platform] using the narrowest permission that works: specific people > people in the organization > anyone with the link (rarely appropriate; never for confidential data).
3. **Set** an expiration date on external shares where the platform supports it.
4. **Check** the sharing setting before sending: the single most common file-sharing failure is a link set to "anyone with the link" on a folder that contains more than the intended file.
5. **Do not** attach a spreadsheet containing hidden tabs, filtered rows, or pivot source data without checking the full contents first.
6. **Do not** email large attachments to distribution groups; use a Shared Drive link.
7. **Scan** any inbound attachment through normal caution rules (SOP-08). **If** you were not expecting a file, **then** verify with the sender by phone before opening.
8. **Do not** open attachments with these extensions unless IT has confirmed them: `.exe`, `.scr`, `.js`, `.vbs`, `.iso`, `.img`, `.hta`, `.lnk`, macro-enabled Office files from external senders, or a password-protected archive where the password was supplied in the same email.

### I.8 — Filing, retention, and deletion
1. **Do not** delete organizational email that documents a decision, a transaction, a service, an incident, or a communication with a regulator, funder, donor, vendor, or individual served.
2. **Delete** only routine transitory messages (meeting logistics, spam, duplicate notifications).
3. **File** by label rather than by folder-moving where possible; Gmail labels preserve searchability.
4. **Recommended label structure** ⬜ *(confirm with Compliance)*:
   `01-Action` · `02-Waiting` · `03-Reference` · `Contracts` · `Finance` · `Grants` · `Incidents` · `Personnel` · `Sites/[Site ID]` · `Vendors/[Vendor]`
5. **Understand** that Google Vault retention operates independently of what you delete from your mailbox. Deleting a message from Gmail does not remove it from the archive during a retention period or hold.
6. **If** you receive a legal hold notice, **then** stop all deletion in the covered scope immediately and confirm receipt in writing to the issuer.
7. **If** you are asked by anyone — including a supervisor or executive — to delete email related to an incident, investigation, complaint, or regulatory matter, **then** do not comply, and report the request to [VP Compliance] and [Legal Counsel Contact] the same day.

### I.9 — Forwarding organizational email to personal accounts
1. **Automatic forwarding of organizational email to any external or personal address is prohibited.** IT will disable auto-forwarding rules organization-wide and will alert on new forwarding rules, which are a primary indicator of account compromise.
2. **Manual forwarding** of an individual message to an external address is permitted **only** when: (a) the message contains no confidential information; and (b) there is a legitimate business reason; and (c) it is not to your own personal account.
3. **Departing employees** may not forward, copy, download, or export organizational email, contacts, calendars, or files to a personal account or device. Personal contacts may be requested through [Human Resources Contact] and are released at the organization's discretion after review.
4. **If** you believe you need forwarding for a business reason, **then** submit a Technology Exception Request (Appendix I). Expect it to be denied absent an unusual circumstance.

### I.10 — Prohibited uses
1. Sending, storing, or requesting material that is discriminatory, harassing, threatening, sexually explicit, or otherwise violates the Employee Handbook.
2. Conducting a personal business, political campaign activity, or solicitation.
3. Sending mass email to staff, families, or donors without authorization from [Marketing/Communications Contact] or [Executive Director/CEO].
4. Sharing your credentials or accessing another person's mailbox without approved delegation.
5. Misrepresenting your identity, role, or authority.
6. Signing contracts, approving payments, or committing organizational funds without documented authority.
7. Using organizational email to communicate with a former employee about confidential organizational matters.
8. Circumventing security controls (disabling banners, whitelisting a blocked sender without IT approval, using a personal account to route around filtering).
9. Auto-forwarding as described in I.9.
10. Storing the only copy of an important organizational record in an individual mailbox rather than a Shared Drive.

### I.11 — Phishing reporting (mandatory)
1. **Stop.** Do not click, reply, open attachments, or enter credentials.
2. **Report within one hour** using Gmail's **Report phishing** option (three-dot menu on the message) **and** by forwarding to ⬜ [PHISHING REPORT ADDRESS].
3. **Do not** forward the suspicious message to coworkers to ask what they think.
4. **If** you already clicked a link or entered your password, **then** stop and follow SOP-08.3 immediately — disconnect, call [IT Support Phone], and report. **You will not be disciplined for reporting this.**
5. **If** the message impersonates an executive and requests money, gift cards, banking changes, or W-2/payroll data, **then** report it to [IT Support Email] **and** [Director of Finance] immediately, and alert your supervisor by phone.
6. **Await** IT's confirmation before treating the message as legitimate. Never rely on your own judgment alone once you have flagged something as suspicious.

### I.12 — Out-of-office messages

**Standard template:**

> Thank you for your message. I am out of the office from **[start date]** and will return on **[return date]**. I will respond when I return.
>
> For immediate assistance, please contact **[Name, Title]** at **[email]** or **[phone]**.
>
> For urgent site or resident matters, contact **[Program/On-Call Line]** at **[Emergency Contact Number]**.
>
> For IT support, submit a request at **[Work Order Submission Link]** or call **[IT Support Phone]**.
>
> [Name] · [Title] · [Organization Name]

**Rules:**
1. **Do not** state where you are traveling, that you are out of the country, or that you have no access to email — this information is used by attackers to time business email compromise attempts.
2. **Do** name a covering colleague with contact details.
3. **Do** set both internal and external versions; keep the external version shorter and less specific.
4. **Turn off** the auto-reply on your first day back.
5. **If** you hold payment-approval authority, **then** notify [Director of Finance] of your absence and coverage directly — do not rely on the auto-reply, because attackers target approvers who are known to be away.

### I.13 — "Before You Send" checklist

> **☐ BEFORE YOU SEND — 8 CHECKS**
> 1. ☐ **Recipients:** Is every address correct? Did autocomplete pick the wrong person?
> 2. ☐ **Reply All:** Does everyone on this thread actually need it?
> 3. ☐ **Confidential content:** Is there PHI, PII, personnel, donor, or financial data? If yes — is this the approved secure method, and does every recipient have a need to know?
> 4. ☐ **Attachments:** Attached what I said I attached? Checked hidden tabs and extra rows? Should this be a link instead?
> 5. ☐ **Sharing permissions:** If I'm sending a link, is it restricted to the right people?
> 6. ☐ **Subject line:** Does it state topic + action + date?
> 7. ☐ **Tone and record:** Am I comfortable with this being read by a surveyor, an attorney, a funder, or the Board?
> 8. ☐ **Money or access:** Does this message request or approve a payment, a banking change, or an access change? If yes — is the SOP-08.6 verification complete?

### I.14 — Standard business email template

```
Subject: [ACTION | DECISION NEEDED | FYI] – [Topic] – [By Date]

[Name],

[One sentence: what this is about.]
[One sentence: what I need from you and by when.]

Background:
1. [Fact]
2. [Fact]
3. [Fact]

Options / Recommendation:
- Option A: [description] — [cost/impact]
- Option B: [description] — [cost/impact]
- Recommendation: [choice and one-line reason]

Next step: [who does what by when]

Thank you,
[Standard signature per Appendix K]
```

## J. Escalation Criteria
| Trigger | Escalate to | Timeframe |
|---|---|---|
| Suspected phishing | [IT Support Email] + report button | 1 hour |
| Clicked a phishing link or entered credentials | [IT Support Phone] by voice | Immediately |
| PHI/PII sent to the wrong recipient | Supervisor → IT → Compliance | 1 hour |
| Suspected mailbox compromise (unknown sent items, unexpected forwarding rule, unrecognized login) | [IT Support Phone] + [Emergency IT Contact] | Immediately |
| Executive impersonation requesting payment or data | IT + [Director of Finance] + supervisor | Immediately |
| Legal, subpoena, or media contact | [Legal Counsel Contact] / [Marketing Contact] + COO | Same day |
| Request to delete records | [VP Compliance] + [Legal Counsel Contact] | Same day |
| Mailbox unavailable at a site affecting operations | [Work Order Submission Link], priority High | 1 hour |

## K. Required Documentation and Record Retention
| Document | Owner | Retention |
|---|---|---|
| Email content (business records) | User / Vault | 7 years ⬜ *(Legal to confirm)* |
| Phishing reports and dispositions | IT | 7 years |
| Misdirected-email incident reports | Compliance | 6 years from resolution (HIPAA) |
| Mailbox delegation approvals | IT | Duration + 7 years |
| Legal hold notices and releases | Legal | Permanent |
| Distribution list membership changes | IT | 3 years |

## L. Service-Level Target *(proposed targets requiring leadership approval)*
| Service | Proposed target |
|---|---|
| New mailbox provisioned after approved request | 1 business day before start date |
| Mailbox delegation configured | 1 business day |
| Phishing report triaged by IT/MSP | 4 business hours |
| Confirmed malicious message purged organization-wide | 8 business hours |
| Mailbox restore from Vault (routine) | 3 business days |
| Suspected account compromise contained | 1 hour from report, 24/7 |

## M. Quality-Control Checklist
- ☐ Every active employee has exactly one organizational mailbox; no shared individual credentials exist
- ☐ Auto-forwarding to external domains is disabled organization-wide and alerting is enabled
- ☐ External-sender banner is active on all inbound mail
- ☐ SPF, DKIM, and DMARC are configured and DMARC is at enforcement ⬜ *(confirm posture with MSP)*
- ☐ Vault retention rules match the approved retention schedule
- ☐ Distribution groups reviewed quarterly for stale members
- ☐ Shared mailboxes have a named owner and documented member list
- ☐ Phishing report address is monitored and has a documented backup monitor
- ☐ Out-of-office templates in use do not disclose travel
- ☐ Signature standard (Appendix K) applied organization-wide

## N. Related Policies and Forms
SOP-02 (Password/MFA) · SOP-07 (Acceptable Use) · SOP-08 (Cybersecurity) · SOP-09 (Access) · SOP-14 (Data Classification) · SOP-21 (Retention & Legal Hold) · SOP-23 (External Communications) · Appendix E, K, L · HIPAA Privacy & Security Policies · Employee Handbook

## O. Revision Notes
| Version | Date | Change |

# SOP-02 — LOST PASSWORD, ACCOUNT LOCKOUT, AND MULTI-FACTOR AUTHENTICATION SUPPORT

**B. SOP Number:** ITG-SOP-02 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

## C. Purpose
1. Restore account access quickly for legitimate users without creating a path an attacker can use.
2. Establish identity verification that cannot be satisfied by information an attacker can find or guess.
3. Eliminate password sharing, which is the single most common control failure in organizations of our size.
4. Ensure that account-recovery requests involving privileged, finance, executive, or clinical accounts receive elevated scrutiny.

> ## ⛔ ABSOLUTE RULE
> **A password will never be reset based solely on an email request, a chat message, or a text message.**
> Every reset requires live voice or video identity verification against the standard in I.2. This rule applies to every account, every requester, and every level of the organization, including the CEO. Anyone who asks IT or the MSP to bypass it is either mistaken or attempting fraud, and the request itself is reportable under SOP-08.

## D. Scope
All organizational accounts: Google Workspace, [Work Order System], finance and accounting systems, payroll, HR, EHR/clinical documentation, billing, VoIP administration, remote access, building access, and all cloud applications on the approved software register.

## E. Owner
**Accountable:** [Chief Operating Officer] · **Responsible:** [Director of Information Technology] · **Delegated execution:** [IT Provider Name] helpdesk

## F. Required Systems, Tools, Forms, or Links
| Item | Reference |
|---|---|
| Self-service reset | ⬜ [INSERT SELF-SERVICE PASSWORD RESET URL, if enabled] |
| Support intake | [Work Order Submission Link] · [IT Support Phone] · [IT Support Email] |
| After-hours / emergency | [Emergency IT Contact] |
| MFA method | Google 2-Step Verification (authenticator app, prompt, or security key) |
| Identity verification source of truth | HR system of record ⬜ [INSERT SYSTEM NAME] |
| Ticket record | Every reset must have a ticket — no exceptions |

## G. Definitions
| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Account lockout** | Automatic temporary block, typically after repeated failed sign-ins. |
| **Temporary credential** | A one-time password issued by IT that must be changed at first sign-in. |
| **Out-of-band verification** | Confirming identity through a channel different from the one the request arrived on. |
| **Privileged account** | Administrative account over systems, data, or other accounts. |
| **Break-glass account** | An emergency administrative account held under seal for use only when normal access is impossible. |
| **Backup codes** | One-time codes issued at MFA enrollment for use when the primary MFA device is unavailable. |

## H. Roles and Responsibilities
| Role | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| **Employee** | Keep your password private; enroll MFA and store backup codes securely; report lost MFA devices immediately; never ask anyone for their password or give yours |
| **Supervisor** | Confirm employment status when asked by IT; never request a reset on an employee's behalf as a substitute for the employee's own verified request; request delegation rather than credentials for coverage |
| **HR** | Maintain the authoritative employment record used for verification; notify IT of status changes |
| **IT / MSP helpdesk** | Verify identity per I.2 before any reset; issue temporary credentials through approved channels only; document every action in the ticket |
| **Director of IT** | Approve resets for privileged accounts; investigate anomalies; review the reset log monthly |
| **COO** | Approve executive account recovery; receive escalation on suspected compromise |

## I. Procedure

### I.1 — How to request help (staff-facing)
1. **Try** self-service reset first at ⬜ [SELF-SERVICE URL], if enabled for your account.
2. **If** self-service does not work or is not available, **then** call **[IT Support Phone]** during business hours.
3. **If** it is outside business hours and you cannot perform your job duties without access, **then** call **[Emergency IT Contact]**.
4. **Submit** a work order at [Work Order Submission Link] as a record — but **do not wait** for the ticket if you are locked out of a system needed for immediate care, medication documentation, or emergency response. Call.
5. **Have ready** when you call: your full name, job title, department, site, supervisor's name, the account/system affected, and the error message you are seeing.
6. **Expect** to be asked to verify your identity. **If** you cannot verify, **then** IT will not reset the account, and you will be routed to in-person verification or supervisor-witnessed verification. This is the control working correctly.
7. **Never** email or text your password, and **never** ask IT to email you a working password.

### I.2 — Identity verification standard (IT-facing, mandatory)

Before any credential action, the technician must complete verification at the level required by the account tier.

| Account tier | Minimum verification | Approval required |
|---|---|---|
| **Tier 1 — Standard user** (email, files, work order, timekeeping) | Live voice call on a number listed in the HR/directory record **plus** two of: employee ID, hire date, supervisor name, last four of employee number, a recent ticket number **or** a callback to the directory number | Technician |
| **Tier 2 — Sensitive user** (HR, billing, EHR/clinical, payroll, donor database, anyone with payment-initiation ability) | Tier 1 **plus** video verification or supervisor confirmation on a separate call | Senior technician or Director of IT |
| **Tier 3 — Privileged / administrative** (domain admin, Google super admin, financial system admin, VoIP admin, MSP admin, security tooling) | Tier 2 **plus** written approval from [Director of Information Technology] **plus** callback on a pre-registered number | Director of IT |
| **Tier 4 — Executive** (CEO, COO, CFO/Director of Finance, CHRO, CCO, Board officers) | Tier 2 **plus** approval from [Director of Information Technology] **and** one other executive, on a live call | Director of IT + second executive |

**Mandatory technician rules:**
1. **Call back** on a number from the authoritative directory. Never accept a callback number supplied in the request itself.
2. **Never** accept caller ID as proof of identity — it is trivially spoofed.
3. **Never** accept an email chain, a forwarded approval, or a Teams/Chat message as verification.
4. **If** the caller is hostile, in a hurry, invokes an executive's authority, or pressures you to skip a step, **then** stop, complete verification anyway, and flag the ticket for security review. Urgency and authority pressure are the two signatures of a social-engineering attempt.
5. **Do not** disclose to a caller which verification questions they failed.
6. **Record** in the ticket: verification method, data points confirmed, approver, timestamp, technician name.

### I.3 — Password sharing rules
1. **Passwords must never be shared with anyone** — not supervisors, not IT, not the MSP, not executives, not auditors, not family members.
2. **IT will never ask for your password.** Any request for your password is a security incident; report it under SOP-08.
3. **If** a coworker asks for your password, **then** decline and report it to your supervisor and [IT Support Email].
4. **If** a shared workstation or a shared function requires multi-person access (for example, a program office computer, an intake mailbox, a kiosk), **then** the correct solution is a **shared account with named, tracked access** or **mailbox/file delegation** — requested through [Work Order Submission Link] with supervisor approval. It is never one person's individual credentials.
5. **If** a group-home or program site is using a single generic login for multiple staff today, **then** report it through [Work Order Submission Link] as a remediation item. ⬜ **[BASELINE ASSESSMENT REQUIRED — identify all shared/generic accounts before adoption; this is a known finding pattern in 24/7 residential settings and must be sized before a compliance date is set.]**
6. **Never** write a password on paper posted at a workstation, taped under a keyboard, or stored in a shared document, spreadsheet, or note.
7. **Use** the organization's approved password manager ⬜ **[INSERT — recommend deploying one; low cost, high control value]** to store credentials.

### I.4 — Password standards
1. **Use** a passphrase of at least ⬜ **[recommend 15]** characters — a sequence of unrelated words is stronger and easier to remember than a short complex string.
2. **Use** a unique password for every organizational system. Never reuse a password from a personal account.
3. **Change** your password immediately if you suspect it has been exposed.
4. **Do not** use information others can find: names, birthdays, site names, the organization's name, or seasons plus a year.
5. **Routine scheduled expiration is not required** where MFA is enforced and compromise monitoring is in place ⬜ *(confirm posture with MSP and cyber carrier — some policies still mandate rotation)*. Forced frequent rotation drives weaker passwords and post-it notes.
6. **Mandatory reset** is required after: a confirmed or suspected compromise; a phishing credential entry; an IT-issued temporary credential; a return from a long leave where access was suspended; and on discovery of the password in a breach-monitoring feed.

### I.5 — Password reset procedure — staff steps
1. **Call** [IT Support Phone] (or [Emergency IT Contact] after hours).
2. **Complete** identity verification with the technician.
3. **Receive** the temporary credential through the approved channel (I.6) — not by email.
4. **Sign in** within the validity window (I.6.4).
5. **Set** a new password immediately at first sign-in; the system will require it.
6. **Re-enroll or confirm** MFA if prompted.
7. **Confirm** to the technician that you have access, so the ticket can be closed.
8. **If** anything about the process felt unusual — an unexpected call offering help, a link to reset, a request for your old password — **then** stop and report under SOP-08.

### I.6 — Password reset procedure — IT steps
1. **Open** a ticket before taking any action. No ticket, no reset.
2. **Verify** identity per I.2 at the correct tier.
3. **Obtain** the required approval for Tier 2–4 and attach it to the ticket.
4. **Generate** a temporary credential that: is unique, is single-use, expires within ⬜ **[recommend 30–60 minutes]**, and forces a change at first sign-in.
5. **Deliver** the temporary credential by an approved out-of-band channel only:
   - Spoken on the verified live call, or
   - SMS/voice to the phone number **already on file** in the authoritative directory, or
   - Via the approved secure-delivery tool ⬜ [INSERT, if any].
   **Never** by email, never by chat, never to a number provided during the request.
6. **Do not** reveal the previous password — IT cannot and must not retrieve it.
7. **Confirm** the user's successful sign-in and password change before closing.
8. **Review** the account for signs of compromise whenever the reset was triggered by anything other than simple forgetfulness: unrecognized sign-in locations, new forwarding rules, new filters, new app passwords, new delegated access, unexpected MFA enrollments.
9. **If** any indicator is present, **then** do not simply reset — escalate to SOP-08 incident handling.
10. **Document** in the ticket: requester, verification, approver, delivery channel, timestamps, anomaly check result, closure confirmation.

### I.7 — First-login password change requirement
1. Every temporary credential must force a change at first sign-in. No exceptions.
2. The new password must meet I.4 standards and must not match any recent prior password.
3. **If** the user does not sign in before the temporary credential expires, **then** the credential is invalidated and the process restarts at I.6.2 — including re-verification.

### I.8 — Lost, stolen, replaced, or failed MFA device
1. **Report immediately** — call [IT Support Phone], or [Emergency IT Contact] after hours. Do not wait until your next shift.
2. **If** the device is a personal phone that also held organizational email or files, **then** this is also a lost-device incident — follow SOP-12.5 and SOP-08.
3. **IT will:**
   a. Verify identity at the tier required by the account (I.2). Lost-MFA requests are a favored attacker technique, so verification is **elevated one tier** above the account's normal tier.
   b. Revoke the lost authenticator enrollment and any active sessions on that device.
   c. Revoke previously issued backup codes.
   d. Issue a temporary enrollment method with a short validity window.
   e. Require re-enrollment of MFA at first sign-in.
   f. Issue new backup codes and instruct the user on secure storage.
   g. Check the account for indicators of compromise per I.6.8.
4. **If** the phone was lost with the device unlocked, in a public place, or under circumstances suggesting theft, **then** treat as a potential compromise: force a password reset, revoke all sessions, and open a security incident.
5. **Backup codes:** store them in the approved password manager or a locked physical location — never in your email, never in a note on the phone that holds the authenticator, never in a photo on that phone.
6. **Planned phone replacement:** submit a work order **before** wiping or trading in the old phone so MFA can be migrated without an emergency reset. **If** the old phone is already gone, **then** follow the lost-device path above.
7. **Temporary MFA bypass is prohibited.** Access is restored by re-enrollment, not by turning MFA off. **If** an operational emergency appears to require a bypass, **then** it is escalated to [Director of Information Technology] and [Chief Operating Officer], who may authorize a time-boxed alternative method — never a disabled control — with a documented expiry not exceeding ⬜ **[recommend 24 hours]**.

### I.9 — Account lockout
1. **Wait** ⬜ **[INSERT LOCKOUT DURATION — recommend 15–30 minutes]**; many lockouts clear automatically.
2. **Check** the common causes first:
   a. Caps Lock on.
   b. An old password saved in a phone, tablet, or second computer that keeps retrying — **this is the number one cause of repeat lockouts.** Update or remove the saved password on every device.
   c. A saved credential in a browser or an email client on a device you rarely use.
   d. A shared workstation where someone else typed your username.
3. **If** it does not clear, **then** call [IT Support Phone].
4. **If** you are locked out repeatedly (three or more times in seven days), **then** IT must investigate the root cause rather than repeatedly unlocking. Repeated lockout is either a device misconfiguration or an attack in progress.
5. **IT:** treat a lockout pattern across multiple users, or lockouts originating from unfamiliar locations, as a potential password-spray attack and escalate under SOP-08 immediately.

### I.10 — Elevated escalation: privileged, finance, executive, and suspected compromise
1. **If** the account is Tier 3 or Tier 4, **then** the reset requires the approvals in I.2 and a notification to [Director of Information Technology] regardless of outcome.
2. **If** the account can initiate or approve payments, **then** notify [Director of Finance] of the reset the same day, so that payment activity during the window can be reviewed.
3. **If** compromise is suspected at any point, **then** execute in this order:
   a. Reset the password and **revoke all active sessions and tokens**.
   b. Review and remove unauthorized forwarding rules, filters, delegates, app passwords, OAuth app grants, and MFA enrollments.
   c. Preserve logs before making changes where possible.
   d. Notify [Director of Information Technology] and [Chief Operating Officer].
   e. **If** the account touched PHI, **then** notify [VP Compliance] the same day.
   f. **If** the account touched payment systems, **then** notify [Director of Finance] immediately and review all transactions in the exposure window.
   g. Open a Severity assessment under SOP-08 / SOP-17.
4. **Break-glass accounts:** maintained under seal, tested ⬜ [recommend annually], credentials split between [Director of Information Technology] and [Chief Operating Officer], every use logged and reviewed within 1 business day.

## J. Escalation Criteria
| Trigger | Escalate to | Timeframe |
|---|---|---|
| Identity verification fails | Supervisor + Director of IT | Same call |
| Requester pressures technician to skip verification | Director of IT; log as attempted social engineering | Immediately |
| Reset request for Tier 3/4 account | Director of IT (+ second executive for Tier 4) | Before action |
| Signs of compromise during reset | Director of IT + COO; SOP-08 activated | Immediately |
| Lost MFA device with organizational data on it | IT + Compliance (if PHI) | 1 hour |
| Multiple users locked out simultaneously | Director of IT + MSP; treat as attack until disproven | Immediately |
| MFA bypass requested | Director of IT + COO | Before any action |
| Access needed for immediate resident care and cannot be verified | Site supervisor + on-call manager; use documented downtime procedure (SOP-25) | Immediately |

## K. Required Documentation and Record Retention
| Document | Contents | Retention |
|---|---|---|
| Reset ticket | Requester, account, verification method and data points, approver, delivery channel, timestamps, anomaly check, closure | 7 years |
| MFA re-enrollment record | User, device revoked, method re-enrolled, backup codes issued | 7 years |
| Privileged reset log | All Tier 3/4 actions with dual approval evidence | 7 years |
| Break-glass use log | Who, when, why, what was done, review sign-off | 7 years |
| Attempted social-engineering reports | Description, caller details, disposition | 7 years |
| Monthly reset review | Volume, tier mix, anomalies, repeat users | 3 years |

## L. Service-Level Target *(proposed targets requiring leadership approval)*
| Situation | Proposed response | Proposed resolution |
|---|---|---|
| Standard reset, business hours | 30 minutes | 1 hour |
| Standard reset, after hours (non-critical) | Next business day | Next business day |
| Critical operational lockout (clinical documentation, medication record, payroll cutoff, emergency systems) | 15 minutes, 24/7 | 1 hour |
| Lost MFA device | 1 hour | 4 hours |
| Tier 3/4 privileged or executive reset | 1 hour | 4 hours (approval-dependent) |
| Suspected compromise | Immediate | Containment within 1 hour |

## M. Quality-Control Checklist
- ☐ 100% of resets have a ticket with documented verification
- ☐ Zero resets executed on email-only requests (audited monthly)
- ☐ Temporary credentials expire within the approved window and force change at first login
- ☐ MFA enforced on all accounts with access to email, PHI, finance, payroll, or HR data
- ☐ No generic/shared individual credentials in use, or all exceptions registered
- ☐ Backup-code issuance and storage guidance delivered at every enrollment
- ☐ Monthly review of the reset log for anomalies and repeat lockouts completed
- ☐ Break-glass credentials tested and re-sealed within the past 12 months
- ☐ Quick-reference card (QR-5) posted at every site and shared workstation

# SOP-03 — WORK ORDER CREATION AND SUPPORT REQUEST PROCESS

**B. SOP Number:** ITG-SOP-03 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

---

> # 📝 SUBMIT A WORK ORDER
>
> ## 👉 **[WORK ORDER SUBMISSION LINK]** 👈
>
> **This is the only approved way to request IT, Facilities, or Fleet help.**
>
> | | |
> |---|---|
> | **Submit a work order** | **[Work Order Submission Link]** |
> | **IT support email** | [IT Support Email] |
> | **IT support phone** | [IT Support Phone] |
> | **After-hours emergency IT** | [Emergency IT Contact] |
> | **Life safety, fire, medical emergency** | **CALL 911 FIRST** |
> | **Program / on-call emergency line** | [Emergency Contact Number] |
>
> **⬜ Before adoption:** the link above must be live, tested from a personal phone on cellular data, tested from a shared site workstation, and printed on a card posted at every site. A work-order policy with a broken link trains staff to call people directly, and the organization loses its only record of what is breaking.

---

## C. Purpose
1. Give every employee one front door for technology, facilities, and fleet problems.
2. Create a complete, searchable record of what breaks, where, how often, and at what cost.
3. Ensure requests are prioritized by operational and resident impact rather than by who asks loudest.
4. Give leadership the data to see recurring failures, vendor performance, and capital replacement needs.

> **Why one intake for IT, Facilities, and Fleet.** These functions report to the same executive and share the same fundamental data: a site, an asset, a problem, a responder, a cost, and a resolution. One intake and one data model means a boiler, a laptop, a van, and a printer all build asset history in the same place — which is what makes lifecycle planning (SOP-22), vendor scorecards (SOP-16), and capital budgeting possible without a separate reporting project. Two ticket systems would produce two truths and no capital plan.

## D. Scope
All requests for: IT support, telephony, network, printing, accounts and access, software, hardware; facilities and building maintenance; grounds; fleet and vehicle maintenance; equipment; and property-related issues at all sites in the Section 2.3 roster.

**Out of scope for work orders — these use their own channels:**
1. Life-safety emergencies → **911**, then notify per program policy.
2. Resident incidents → **UIR process**, per program policy.
3. Employee relations and HR matters → [Human Resources Contact].
4. Clinical or medical concerns → clinical chain of command.
5. Suspected cybersecurity incidents → **call [IT Support Phone] immediately**; a work order alone is not sufficient (SOP-08).

## E. Owner
**Accountable:** [Chief Operating Officer] · **Responsible (IT queue):** [Director of Information Technology] · **Responsible (Facilities/Fleet queue):** [Director of Facilities & Fleet Management] · **Consulted:** VP of Operations
