# SOP-10 — ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE USAGE STANDARD AND GOVERNANCE

**B. SOP Number:** ITG-SOP-10 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

## C. Purpose
1. Let staff use AI to do more with the capacity we have, which in a rate-constrained, labor-intensive organization is a real operating advantage.
2. Prevent resident, employee, donor, and financial information from being entered into tools the organization does not control and has no agreement with.
3. Ensure decisions about people's services, housing, benefits, health, employment, and money remain human decisions with human accountability.
4. Establish an approval path so that staff have a legitimate way to get the tools they need — because a policy that only says "no" produces shadow AI, which is worse than governed AI.

> **Governing sentence for this entire SOP:**
> **AI may help you draft, summarize, organize, and think. It may never decide anything about a person, and no confidential information about a real person ever goes into it unless the tool is on the approved list and approved for that data type.**

## D. Scope
All use of artificial intelligence for organizational purposes, by any person covered by this manual, on any device, using any account — including free tools, personal accounts, AI features embedded inside approved products, browser extensions, and AI features added to existing software by a vendor update.

## E. Owner
**Accountable:** [Chief Operating Officer] · **Responsible:** [Director of Information Technology] · **Governance body:** AI Governance Committee (I.12) · **Consulted:** Compliance, Legal, HR, Clinical, Finance, Development

⬜ **[LEGAL, HR, COMPLIANCE, AND CLINICAL REVIEW REQUIRED BEFORE ADOPTION.]** The prohibitions in I.4 constrain HR, Clinical, and Compliance practice. Per Section 6.2, they must be co-owned by those executives, not issued to them.

## F. Required Systems, Tools, Forms, or Links
[Approved AI Tool List] ⬜ **[BUILD REQUIRED — this list must exist before this SOP takes effect]** · Appendix G (Software/AI Tool Request) · AI tool register ⬜ [INSERT] · [Work Order Submission Link] · data classification standard (SOP-14) · AI incident reporting: [IT Support Email] / [IT Support Phone]

## G. Definitions
| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Artificial intelligence (AI)** | Software that performs tasks normally requiring human judgment — generating text, summarizing, classifying, predicting, recognizing speech or images, or recommending actions. |
| **Generative AI** | AI that produces new content — text, images, audio, video, code, or summaries — in response to a prompt. |
| **Approved AI tool** | A tool on the [Approved AI Tool List], contracted by the organization, reviewed for security and privacy, and approved for specific data classifications. |
| **Public AI tool** | Any AI service not on the approved list, including free consumer versions, personal-account versions of approved products, and AI features in unapproved software. **Assume anything you type into a public AI tool may be retained and used to train the service.** |
| **Embedded AI** | AI capability added inside a product we already use (a meeting transcriber, a summarizer in a document editor, an AI assistant in an EHR). Embedded AI is still AI and is still governed by this SOP. |
| **Confidential information** | Any information that would harm a person or the organization if disclosed. See SOP-14. |
| **PII** | Personally identifiable information. |
| **PHI** | Protected health information under HIPAA — including the fact that a named person receives our services. |
| **Human review** | A qualified person reads the output, verifies its accuracy and appropriateness, corrects it, and takes personal responsibility for it. Skimming is not review. |
| **Automated decision-making** | A system producing an outcome about a person without meaningful human judgment applied. **Prohibited for any decision within this SOP's high-risk list.** |
| **High-risk use** | Any AI use that could affect a person's services, housing, health, safety, benefits, eligibility, employment, discipline, compensation, legal rights, or money. |
| **AI disclosure** | A statement that AI materially contributed to a document. |
| **Hallucination** | Confident, fluent output that is factually wrong or fabricated — including invented citations, regulations, statistics, and quotations. |

## H. Roles and Responsibilities
| Role | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| **Employee** | Use only approved tools; classify data before entering it; review and verify every output; take ownership of anything you send or publish; report problems |
| **Supervisor** | Know which of your staff use AI and for what; review AI-assisted work products in your approval chain; enforce the prohibitions |
| **Director of IT** | Maintain the approved list and register; run vendor assessments; apply technical controls; investigate unauthorized use |
| **AI Governance Committee** | Approve tools and use cases; review incidents; set and revise the risk posture quarterly |
| **COO** | Accountable executive; approves high-risk use cases; reports AI risk to the CEO and Board |
| **Compliance** | PHI and privacy determinations; BAA requirements; regulatory interpretation |
| **Legal** | Contract terms, IP, confidentiality, disclosure requirements, employment-law implications |
| **HR** | Any AI touching recruitment, evaluation, discipline, or compensation — with the constraints in I.4 |
| **Clinical** | Any AI touching clinical content, assessment, or documentation |

## I. Procedure

### I.1 — Guiding principles
1. **People decide. AI assists.** Every output is a draft until a qualified human takes responsibility for it.
2. **Never enter confidential information about a real person into a tool not approved for it.**
3. **Verify every fact.** AI fabricates citations, regulations, statistics, and quotations with complete confidence. Fluency is not accuracy.
4. **Use the approved tool.** If the approved tool is inadequate, request a better one (I.11) — do not use an unapproved one.
5. **Disclose when it matters.** See I.9.
6. **Be alert to bias.** AI reflects patterns in its training data. In an organization serving people with intellectual and developmental disabilities, language that subtly diminishes autonomy or dignity is a real and recurring risk, and it must be caught by human review.
7. **You own the output.** "The AI wrote it" is not a defense for anything sent, published, filed, or submitted under your name or the organization's.
8. **Governed use beats banned use.** A prohibition without an approved alternative produces hidden use and no visibility.

### I.2 — Data classification before use (do this every time)

| If the information is… | Examples | Public AI tool | Approved AI tool (standard) | Approved AI tool **with executed BAA and approved for PHI** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Public** | Published mission statement, public job posting, press release already issued, publicly posted program description | ✅ Permitted | ✅ | ✅ |
| **Internal** | Draft meeting agenda with no personal data, general policy language, a de-identified process description, a generic template | ⚠️ Discouraged — use approved tools | ✅ | ✅ |
| **Confidential** | Employee names in a scheduling context, vendor contract terms, donor names, unpublished financials, site-level operational data | ❌ **Prohibited** | ✅ Permitted | ✅ |
| **Restricted / PHI / sensitive personal** | Any resident information, including first names; diagnoses; behavior plans; incident details; medical or medication information; personnel files; disciplinary records; SSNs; financial account data; credentials | ❌ **Prohibited** | ❌ **Prohibited** | ⬜ Only if explicitly approved for PHI by Compliance **and** covered by an executed BAA **and** approved for the specific use |

> **The IDD-specific trap.** In a small service area, a resident can be re-identified from surprisingly little: "the 34-year-old man at our Closter facility who uses a wheelchair and had a recent hospitalization" is identifiable even with no name. **De-identification means removing everything that lets a reader work out who it is — not just deleting the name.** When in doubt, do not use AI at all; write it yourself.

### I.3 — Approved uses (with approved tools)

| Work product | Permitted use | Required human step |
|---|---|---|
| **Emails** | Draft, shorten, reorganize, adjust tone, reduce reading level | Read every word; verify facts, names, dates, and commitments before sending |
| **Reports and memos** | Structure, draft, summarize your own notes, tighten language | Verify every number against its source; you own the numbers |
| **Policies and procedures** | Draft structure, generate first drafts, identify gaps, improve readability | Legal/Compliance review before adoption; verify every regulatory citation independently |
| **Grant narratives** | Outline, draft narrative sections, tighten to word limits, improve clarity | Verify all data and outcomes; confirm funder AI rules; disclose if required (I.9) |
| **Job descriptions** | Draft, standardize format, improve clarity, remove biased language | HR reviews and owns; verify accuracy of duties and requirements; check for accessibility and inclusion |
| **Meeting notes** | Summarize notes you took; organize action items | Verify accuracy with attendees; **recording or transcribing a meeting requires consent from all participants — see I.4.14** |
| **Research summaries** | Summarize public regulations, industry practice, vendor comparisons | **Verify every citation against the primary source.** AI invents regulation numbers that look real |
| **Contractor scopes of work** | Draft scope, specifications, evaluation criteria | Facilities/Procurement review; verify technical specifications with a qualified person |
| **Facilities documentation** | Draft preventive-maintenance checklists, equipment procedures, inspection forms | Verify against manufacturer documentation and code requirements |
| **Property communications** | Draft notices to landlords, property managers, neighbors, municipalities | Legal review for anything with contractual or regulatory effect |
| **Operational planning** | Structure plans, generate scenarios and options, build timelines, draft KPI definitions | Leadership owns every decision; AI produces options, not choices |
| **Data analysis of de-identified aggregates** | Summarize trends in de-identified, aggregated operational data (work order volume, downtime, cost) | Verify calculations independently; never upload a file containing individual-level personal data |
| **Training materials** | Draft outlines, scenarios, quiz questions | Subject-matter expert review; verify all compliance content |
| **Translation / plain-language rewriting** | Simplify complex material into plain language for staff | Human fluent in the target language verifies; never for clinical instructions or legal notices without qualified review |

### I.4 — Prohibited uses (absolute)

**Never enter into any AI tool:**
1. Resident or individual-served information of any kind, including names, initials, dates, locations, or descriptions that could identify a person.
2. Protected health information: diagnoses, medications, treatment, behavior support plans, clinical notes, incident details, hospitalization information.
3. Employee personnel information: performance reviews, disciplinary records, medical or leave information, compensation, complaints, investigation materials.
4. Social Security numbers, dates of birth, driver's license numbers, or government identifiers.
5. Financial account numbers, routing numbers, credit card data, or banking credentials.
6. Passwords, API keys, security configurations, or any credential.
7. Donor personal or giving information.
8. Unpublished financial statements, Board materials, or legal strategy.
9. Contract terms under active negotiation, without Legal approval.
10. Anything covered by a confidentiality agreement or a legal hold.

**Never use AI to make or materially determine:**
11. Any clinical decision, assessment, diagnosis, treatment plan, medication decision, or behavior support determination.
12. Any employment decision: hiring, screening, ranking, discipline, termination, promotion, or compensation. ⬜ **[HR AND LEGAL REVIEW — automated employment decision tools are increasingly regulated; confirm current New Jersey and federal requirements.]**
13. Any eligibility, benefits, or service-authorization determination for an individual.
14. Any housing decision: placement, transfer, roommate matching, or discharge.
15. Any determination about an individual's rights, restrictions, guardianship, or legal status.
16. Any disciplinary or investigatory conclusion about an employee or an individual served.
17. Any final content of a regulatory submission, a UIR, a survey response, or a legal filing without full qualified human authorship and review.
18. Any decision to deny, reduce, or terminate a service.

**Never do these things:**
19. Use a personal AI account for organizational work.
20. Use an AI tool not on the [Approved AI Tool List].
21. Enable an AI feature inside an existing product without IT approval — including meeting transcription, email summarization, and "smart" assistants added by a vendor update.
22. Record or transcribe a meeting, a call, or a conversation using an AI tool without the express consent of all participants and, where individuals served are present, without program and Compliance approval. ⬜ **[LEGAL REVIEW — New Jersey consent requirements.]**
23. Present AI output as your own original professional work where the distinction matters (clinical assessment, legal analysis, professional certification).
24. Use AI to generate images or video depicting individuals served, or to alter photographs of real people.
25. Use AI to write a reference, an evaluation, or a recommendation about a specific employee.
26. Upload an organizational file, spreadsheet, or document to a public AI tool.

### I.5 — Human review and accountability standard
1. **A named human is accountable for every AI-assisted work product.** That person is the one who sends, submits, publishes, or files it.
2. **Review proportionate to risk:**

| Risk level | Examples | Required review |
|---|---|---|
| **Low** | Internal email draft, formatting cleanup, brainstorming | Author reads and verifies |
| **Moderate** | Internal report, policy draft, job description, vendor scope | Author verifies + supervisor reviews |
| **High** | Grant submission, Board material, external publication, funder communication, anything with regulatory content | Author verifies + department director reviews + subject-matter expert verifies all facts and citations |
| **Prohibited** | Anything in I.4 | Not permitted at any review level |

3. **Verification means:** check every factual claim against a source you can name; check every number against its system of record; check every citation by opening the actual regulation, statute, or document; check every name, date, and dollar figure.
4. **If** you cannot verify a claim in the output, **then** delete it. Do not publish something you cannot stand behind.
5. **Never** paste AI output directly into a system of record, a regulatory submission, or a resident record.

### I.6 — Fact-checking, sources, bias, privacy, and intellectual property
1. **Citations:** AI regularly fabricates regulation numbers, case names, statistics, and quotations that look entirely plausible. **Open the primary source every time.** A citation you did not personally open does not go into an organizational document.
2. **Currency:** AI tools have knowledge cutoffs and may not reflect current New Jersey rules, DDD policy, Medicaid rates, or regulatory changes. Never rely on AI for a current rate, rule, or deadline.
3. **Bias:** review output for language that stereotypes disability, diminishes autonomy, uses outdated or person-second language, or reflects assumptions about race, age, sex, religion, national origin, or disability. **Use person-first or identity-first language per organizational standard** ⬜ [INSERT ORGANIZATIONAL LANGUAGE STANDARD].
4. **Privacy:** assume anything entered into a public tool is retained. Even in approved enterprise tools, enter only what the tool is approved to receive.
5. **Intellectual property:**
   a. AI output may reproduce copyrighted material. Do not publish AI-generated text or images without review.
   b. Do not upload third-party copyrighted material, licensed content, or another organization's confidential documents into AI tools.
   c. Ownership of AI output is legally unsettled; do not assume the organization holds copyright in AI-generated material. ⬜ **[LEGAL REVIEW before using AI-generated material in branded, licensed, or commercially significant work.]**
   d. Do not use AI to generate anything resembling another organization's protected branding.
6. **Accuracy in funder and regulatory contexts:** an inaccurate statement in a grant report or a regulatory submission is a compliance matter regardless of how it was drafted.

### I.7 — Data classification decision: "Before You Use AI" checklist

> **☐ BEFORE YOU USE AI — 7 CHECKS**
> 1. ☐ **Is this tool on the [Approved AI Tool List]?** If no → stop. Request it via Appendix G.
> 2. ☐ **Am I signed in with my organizational account?** Personal accounts are not permitted.
> 3. ☐ **Does anything I'm about to enter identify a real person?** A name, initials, a date, a site, a diagnosis, a description someone local could decode → **stop**.
> 4. ☐ **Is this a decision about a person's services, health, housing, benefits, money, or job?** → **stop**. AI does not decide.
> 5. ☐ **Can I verify every fact the AI gives me?** If no, I will delete what I cannot verify.
> 6. ☐ **Who reviews this before it leaves my hands?** (Name them — see I.5.2.)
> 7. ☐ **Does this need a disclosure?** (See I.9.)
>
> **If you answered "I'm not sure" to any of these: don't use AI for this task. Ask [IT Support Email] or your supervisor.**

### I.8 — AI Use Decision Tree

```
I WANT TO USE AI FOR A TASK
│
├─ Does the task involve deciding something about a real person's
│  services, health, housing, benefits, employment, discipline, money,
│  or legal rights?
│     └─ YES → ❌ STOP. Prohibited. Do the work with human judgment.
│              (AI may still help you format a document AFTER the
│               decision is made and written by a human.)
│
├─ Will I need to enter information that identifies a real person —
│  a resident, an employee, a donor — even indirectly?
│     ├─ YES → Is the tool approved FOR THAT DATA TYPE (PHI-approved,
│     │        BAA executed, Compliance sign-off)?
│     │          ├─ NO  → ❌ STOP. De-identify completely, or do it manually.
│     │          └─ YES → ⚠️ Proceed with minimum necessary data + full review
│     └─ NO  → continue
│
├─ Is the tool on the [Approved AI Tool List]?
│     ├─ NO  → ❌ STOP. Submit Appendix G to request it. Do not use it meanwhile.
│     └─ YES → continue
│
├─ Am I signed in with my ORGANIZATIONAL account?
│     ├─ NO  → ❌ Sign in properly. Personal accounts are prohibited for work.
│     └─ YES → continue
│
├─ What is the risk level of the output? (I.5.2)
│     ├─ LOW      → Draft → I verify → send
│     ├─ MODERATE → Draft → I verify → supervisor reviews → send
│     └─ HIGH     → Draft → I verify every fact and citation →
│                   director reviews → subject-matter expert verifies →
│                   disclosure if required (I.9) → send
│
└─ ALWAYS: verify every fact · open every citation · check for bias and
           dignity in language · a named human owns the result
```

### I.9 — Disclosure requirements

⬜ **[POLICY DECISION REQUIRED — leadership must decide whether to adopt a disclosure requirement, and in what form. The recommendation below is a proposal.]**

**Recommended position:** disclose when AI **materially contributed** to the substance of a document — not when it was used for spelling, formatting, or minor rewording.

| Context | Recommended requirement |
|---|---|
| **Grant applications and reports** | Follow the funder's rule first. **If** the funder requires disclosure or prohibits AI, **then** comply exactly. **If** the funder is silent, **then** ⬜ [recommend internal disclosure to Development and Finance; no external statement] |
| **Board materials** | Disclose to the preparer's executive; note in the document footer where AI produced substantive analysis |
| **Legal documents and regulatory submissions** | **Disclose to Legal/Compliance always.** AI may not draft substantive content without their approval |
| **External publications, website, press, social media** | Disclose per ⬜ [Marketing standard]; never publish AI-generated images depicting people without disclosure and approval |
| **Clinical and program documentation** | AI may not contribute substantively; no disclosure applies because the use is prohibited |
| **Internal operational documents** | No disclosure required |
| **Communications to families, guardians, or individuals served** | ⬜ [recommend no AI-drafted substantive content; human authorship required] |

**Sample disclosure language:**
> *"Portions of this document were drafted with the assistance of an approved artificial intelligence tool. All content has been reviewed, verified, and approved by [Name, Title], who is responsible for its accuracy."*

### I.10 — Permitted, restricted, and prohibited prompt examples

**✅ PERMITTED**
1. "Rewrite this paragraph about our day program model at an eighth-grade reading level. [paste paragraph containing no personal information]"
2. "Draft an outline for a facilities preventive maintenance checklist for commercial HVAC units in residential buildings."
3. "Summarize the key themes in these de-identified work order categories and volumes: [paste aggregate counts only]."
4. "Draft a scope of work for a snow removal contract covering 14 residential properties, including service triggers, response times, insurance requirements, and liability terms."
5. "Give me five interview questions that assess a candidate's experience supervising 24/7 staffing schedules."
6. "Help me structure a one-page executive update with the sections: situation, what changed, what I need from you."
7. "Draft a job description for a Facilities Coordinator. Duties: [list]. Requirements: [list]."
8. "What are common approaches nonprofits use to reduce overtime in 24/7 staffing models? Give sources I can verify."
9. "Turn these meeting notes I typed into an action item list with owners and dates: [notes with no personal or clinical content]."
10. "Draft a plain-language internal notice explaining that the phone system will be upgraded on Saturday and what staff should expect."

**⚠️ RESTRICTED — approved tool, extra care, review required**
11. "Review this draft vendor contract summary for missing terms." *(Confidential — approved tool only; Legal reviews the result; do not paste terms under active negotiation without Legal approval.)*
12. "Summarize these de-identified incident trend counts by category and month." *(Approved tool only; confirm the data truly cannot re-identify anyone; Compliance consulted.)*
13. "Help me draft talking points for a staff meeting about upcoming schedule changes." *(Approved tool; no employee names; HR reviews if it touches terms and conditions of employment.)*
14. "Summarize this published DDD policy document and list what it requires of providers." *(Approved tool; **verify every requirement against the actual policy** — do not rely on the summary.)*
15. "Draft a donor thank-you letter template." *(Approved tool; no donor names or amounts in the prompt; Development reviews.)*

**❌ PROHIBITED**
16. "Here are the notes from [Resident Name]'s behavior support meeting — summarize them." *(PHI. Absolutely prohibited.)*
17. "Based on these incident reports, which residents are highest risk?" *(PHI + prohibited high-risk determination.)*
18. "Write a termination letter for [Employee Name] based on these performance notes." *(Personnel data + prohibited employment decision.)*
19. "Rank these five job applicants and tell me who to hire." *(Prohibited employment decision.)*
20. "Which of our residents should be moved to a different home?" *(Prohibited housing decision + PHI.)*
21. "Here is our bank account and routing number — draft a payment instruction letter." *(Financial account data.)*
22. "Draft a response to this DDD survey citation and cite the applicable regulations." *(Regulatory submission; AI-invented citations; requires Compliance authorship.)*
23. "Summarize this employee's medical leave paperwork." *(Personnel + medical data.)*
24. "Here's our Board's executive session material — summarize the key risks." *(Confidential governance material.)*
25. "Transcribe and summarize this recording of our team meeting." *(Requires consent from all participants and IT approval of the tool; prohibited without both.)*
26. "Write a clinical justification for continued ICF placement for this individual." *(Clinical decision + PHI.)*

### I.11 — AI tool approval process
1. **Submit** Appendix G with: the tool, the business problem, the decision it improves, who would use it, what data it would touch, expected cost, and what it replaces.
2. **Screen** (IT, 5 business days): is an approved tool already capable of this? **If** yes, **then** the request is redirected — do not add a second tool for a capability we already pay for.
3. **Assess** the vendor (I.12) — required for any tool touching confidential data.
4. **Classify** the maximum data sensitivity the tool may receive.
5. **Review** by the AI Governance Committee: business value, risk, cost, overlap, exit cost.
6. **Approve** per authority:

| Tool type | Approver |
|---|---|
| No confidential data, no cost, no integration | [Director of Information Technology] |
| Confidential data, or annual cost under ⬜ [INSERT] | [Director of Information Technology] + [Chief Operating Officer] |
| PHI, or annual cost above ⬜ [INSERT], or integration with a core system | AI Governance Committee + [Chief Operating Officer] + [VP Compliance] |
| Any tool supporting a high-risk use case | + [Executive Director/CEO] + [Legal Counsel Contact] |

7. **Execute** contracts, BAA where PHI is involved, and security review before enabling.
8. **Add** to the [Approved AI Tool List] and the AI tool register with: tool, owner, approved uses, approved data classification, users, cost, contract dates, renewal date, and review date.
9. **Train** users before access is granted.
10. **Review** every approved tool ⬜ [recommend annually] for continued need, actual usage, cost, and changed vendor terms. **AI vendor terms change frequently; a tool approved last year may have changed its data-retention or training practices.**
11. **Remove** unused tools and reclaim their cost (SOP-13, SOP-22).

### I.12 — Vendor assessment requirements for AI tools

Required before approval of any AI tool touching confidential data:

| Question | Acceptable answer |
|---|---|
| Is our data used to train the vendor's models? | **No**, contractually, for any tool touching confidential data |
| Where is data stored and processed? | ⬜ [INSERT requirement — recommend United States] |
| What is the data retention period, and can we set it to zero/minimal? | Documented and configurable |
| Can we delete our data on request and on termination? | Yes, with a documented process and timeframe |
| Will the vendor sign a BAA? | **Required** for any PHI use; if no BAA, no PHI, no exceptions |
| Is there SOC 2 Type II or equivalent independent assurance? | Preferred; note any gap as a risk |
| Is MFA and SSO supported? | Required |
| What are the vendor's breach notification commitments? | Documented, with a stated timeframe |
| Does the vendor use subprocessors, and are they disclosed? | Disclosed list required |
| What are the model's known limitations and error rates? | Documented; relevant to the intended use |
| Can we export our data and prompts? | Yes — data portability is an exit-cost question |
| What happens at termination? | Documented deletion and export terms |
| What is the total cost of ownership over 3 years, including per-seat growth? | Modeled before approval |
| What is the exit cost if we leave in year 2? | Estimated before approval |
| Does the tool make or recommend decisions about people? | If yes → high-risk; requires the full approval path |
| Is the vendor financially stable? | Assessed; AI vendors fail and are acquired frequently |

> **Platform note for leadership.** AI capability is increasingly bundled into platforms we already own. Before buying a standalone AI product, confirm whether the capability already exists in the Google Workspace tier we hold or in an existing application. At this scale, the cheapest AI capability is usually the one already inside a platform we pay for, and the most expensive is a collection of departmental point tools nobody inventoried.

### I.13 — Training requirements
1. **Before** any AI tool access: complete the AI Use Standard training ⬜ [recommend 30 minutes].
2. **Content:** what AI is and is not; the data classification rules; the prohibited list; the decision tree; verification and citation checking; bias and dignity in language; disclosure; how to report a problem; hands-on practice with the approved tool.
3. **Annual** refresher, updated for tool and regulatory changes.
4. **Role-specific:** Development (grant and funder AI rules) · HR (employment-decision restrictions) · Clinical (clinical prohibition) · Finance (fraud risk from AI-generated communications, including voice cloning of executives) · Executives (disclosure, Board materials, deepfake and impersonation risk).
5. **Track** completion; report in the executive dashboard.

### I.14 — Reporting AI errors, harmful outputs, privacy concerns, or unauthorized use
1. **Report** to [IT Support Email] or [Work Order Submission Link], category `IT – Security Concern`.
2. **Report immediately by phone** ([IT Support Phone]) if confidential information was entered into an unapproved tool — this may be a reportable privacy incident and time matters.
3. **Report** these specifically:
   a. Confidential or personal information entered into any AI tool in error.
   b. Output that was materially wrong and was used or nearly used.
   c. Output that was biased, discriminatory, or demeaning toward people with disabilities or any protected group.
   d. An AI tool in use that is not on the approved list.
   e. An AI feature that appeared in an existing product without approval.
   f. A suspected AI-generated phishing message, voice call, or video impersonating a staff member. *(Voice cloning of executives is an active and growing fraud technique. The SOP-08.6 verification rule is the defense: verify by calling a known number, not by recognizing a voice.)*
   g. A vendor using AI on our data without disclosure.
4. **Do not** delete the prompt or output — it is the evidence.
5. **IT** logs every report, assesses whether it is a privacy incident (with Compliance), and reports quarterly to the AI Governance Committee.
6. **No discipline** for good-faith reporting of an AI mistake, consistent with Section 3 Principle 11.

### I.15 — Record retention for AI use
1. **Retain** AI-assisted final work products under the same retention schedule as the equivalent non-AI record (SOP-21). The method of drafting does not change the record's retention.
2. **Retain** prompts and outputs where AI materially contributed to: grant submissions, Board materials, policy documents, external publications, and anything subject to a disclosure requirement ⬜ [recommend 3 years].
3. **Retain** AI tool approvals, vendor assessments, and BAAs for the life of the tool plus 7 years.
4. **Retain** AI incident reports for 7 years.
5. **Preserve** AI prompts and outputs under any applicable legal hold — they are discoverable records.
6. **Do not** retain any prompt or output containing PHI entered in error; preserve it only as directed by Compliance for the incident investigation, then delete per their instruction.

### I.16 — Executive oversight and governance

**AI Governance Committee**
1. **Chair:** [Chief Operating Officer].
2. **Members:** Director of IT, VP Compliance, CHRO or designee, Clinical designee, Director of Finance, Director of Development, and ⬜ [recommend one frontline supervisor — the committee will make better rules if someone who does the work is in the room].
3. **Cadence:** ⬜ [recommend quarterly; monthly for the first two quarters].
4. **Standing agenda:** tool register and cost; new requests; incidents and near-misses; usage data; training completion; regulatory and vendor-term changes; unauthorized-use findings; policy revisions.
5. **Reports to:** [Executive Director/CEO]; annually to the Board.

**Periodic risk review** ⬜ [recommend annually]
1. Re-assess every approved tool against I.12.
2. Re-assess the prohibited list against regulatory developments.
3. Review incidents and near-misses for patterns.
4. Review total AI spend against realized benefit — **if a tool cannot be tied to a decision that got faster, better, or cheaper, it is a candidate for removal.**
5. Report findings and recommended changes to the CEO.

**Executive dashboard metrics:** see Part IV — AI tool count and cost, training completion, incidents, unauthorized-use findings, and documented benefit.

## J. Escalation Criteria
| Trigger | Escalate to | Timeframe |
|---|---|---|
| PHI or personal data entered into an AI tool | [IT Support Phone] + Compliance | Immediately, ≤1 hour |
| AI output used in a decision covered by I.4 | COO + the affected function's executive + Legal | Immediately; decision reversed and re-made by human process |
| Unapproved AI tool found in organizational use | Director of IT → COO | 1 business day |
| Biased or harmful output that reached a stakeholder | COO + CHRO/Compliance as applicable | 1 business day |
| Suspected AI-generated impersonation of an executive | [IT Support Phone] + Director of Finance + COO | Immediately |
| Funder or regulator asks about our AI use | COO + Legal + Development | Same day |
| AI vendor changes data-use or training terms | Director of IT → AI Governance Committee | 5 business days |

## K. Required Documentation and Record Retention
See I.15. Plus: approved tool list with version history (permanent) · vendor assessments (life of tool + 7 years) · BAAs (7 years after termination) · committee minutes and decisions (7 years) · training records (7 years after separation) · incident reports (7 years).

## L. Service-Level Target *(proposed, requiring leadership approval)*
| Activity | Target |
|---|---|
| Initial screening of an AI tool request | 5 business days |
| Full vendor assessment | 20 business days |
| Committee decision after complete assessment | Next scheduled meeting, or 30 days |
| Response to an AI misuse report | 1 business day (immediately if PHI involved) |
| Annual tool review completion | Within 30 days of the anniversary |
| Training completion before access | 100%, before access is enabled |

## M. Quality-Control Checklist
- ☐ [Approved AI Tool List] published, current, and accessible to all staff
- ☐ Every approved tool has a named owner, an approved data classification, and a review date
- ☐ BAA on file for every tool approved to touch PHI; zero PHI tools without one
- ☐ 100% of users trained before access
- ☐ Decision tree and Before-You-Use-AI checklist posted where staff work
- ☐ Quarterly discovery scan for unapproved AI tools completed
- ☐ AI incidents logged and reviewed quarterly
- ☐ Embedded AI features inventoried across existing applications (⬜ recommend an initial sweep — this is where unapproved AI usually enters)
- ☐ Annual vendor re-assessment completed for every tool
- ☐ AI spend reconciled to documented benefit annually

## N. Related Policies and Forms
SOP-07 · SOP-08 · SOP-13 · SOP-14 · SOP-16 · SOP-21 · SOP-23 · Appendix G · QR-7 · HIPAA Privacy & Security Policies

# PART III — SUPPORTING STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES

> These SOPs use the same fifteen-part structure in condensed form. They are operationally complete but deliberately shorter than Part II, which carries the procedures frontline staff use daily.

# SOP-11 — IT ASSET INVENTORY, DEVICE ASSIGNMENT, RETURN, REPLACEMENT, AND DISPOSAL

**B.** ITG-SOP-11 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Know what we own, where it is, who has it, what condition it is in, and when it must be replaced — so that devices are secured, budgets are predictable, and no device leaves the organization carrying data.

**D. Scope.** All computers, tablets, mobile phones, hotspots, printers/MFDs, monitors, peripherals, network equipment, VoIP phones, and any device that stores or accesses organizational data. Coordinates with Facilities and Fleet asset records under the shared data model in SOP-03.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] · Consulted: Finance (capitalization), Facilities (site custody)

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Asset inventory system ⬜ [INSERT — one system, not a spreadsheet per department] · Appendix F (Asset Assignment) · [Work Order Submission Link] · disposal vendor ⬜ [INSERT] · device management platform ⬜ [INSERT]

**G. Definitions.** *Asset tag* — the unique organizational label affixed to a device. *Custodian* — the person accountable for a device's physical location. *Sanitization* — the verified destruction of data on media. *Certificate of destruction* — a vendor's documented proof that media was destroyed.

**H. Roles.**
| Role | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| Employee | Safeguard assigned devices; report loss, theft, or damage immediately; return on separation or request |
| Supervisor | Know what devices are at your site; support annual physical verification; recover devices at separation |
| IT | Maintain the single inventory; tag, encrypt, and enroll every device; verify annually; manage disposal |
| Finance | Reconcile inventory to the fixed-asset ledger; fund the replacement plan |

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Record** every device at acquisition with: asset tag, type, make, model, serial, purchase date, cost, funding source, warranty end, assigned user or site, custodian, encryption status, management-enrollment status, and expected replacement date.
2. **Tag** every device physically before deployment.
3. **Encrypt** every device that can store data, before assignment. **No device is assigned unencrypted.**
4. **Enroll** every device in the management platform so it can be located, locked, and wiped remotely.
5. **Assign** via Appendix F; the employee signs; the record updates automatically or within 1 business day.
6. **Assign a custodian for shared and site devices** — a shared program computer with no named custodian is a device nobody will notice is missing.
7. **Report** loss, theft, or damage immediately per SOP-08.2.14. **If** the device held or accessed confidential data, **then** notify Compliance the same day.
8. **Return** devices at separation, role change, or replacement; verify against Appendix F; update the record.
9. **Verify physically** once per year at each site: match every asset tag to the inventory; investigate every discrepancy; document the reconciliation.
10. **Replace** on the lifecycle schedule (SOP-22), not on failure. Failure-driven replacement costs more in downtime than it saves in capital.
11. **Dispose** only through IT:
    a. Remove the device from service and from the network.
    b. Sanitize or physically destroy all storage media — **including printers, MFDs, and scanners, which retain document images.**
    c. Obtain a certificate of destruction listing serial numbers.
    d. Remove the asset from the management platform and mark it disposed in the inventory, with date, method, vendor, and certificate reference.
    e. Notify Finance for the fixed-asset ledger.
12. **Never** sell, donate, discard, or give a device to an employee without completing step 11. ⬜ **[If the organization wishes to donate retired equipment, build that path with Compliance and Finance — it is achievable, but only through the sanitization and certificate process.]**

**J. Escalation.** Lost/stolen device → [IT Support Phone] immediately, Compliance same day if data involved · Unreconciled asset at annual verification → Director of IT → COO · Device disposed without a certificate → COO + Compliance, treat as a potential data incident · Unencrypted device found in use → remove from service immediately.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Inventory record (life of asset + 7 years) · Appendix F assignments (7 years) · disposal certificates (7 years) · annual verification reports (7 years) · loss/theft reports (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed)*. Inventory updated within 1 business day of any change · Device assigned within 3 business days of an approved request · Remote lock/wipe of a lost device within 1 hour of report · Annual verification completed at 100% of sites.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ One inventory system, no departmental shadow lists ☐ 100% of devices tagged ☐ 100% encrypted ☐ 100% enrolled in management ☐ Every shared device has a named custodian ☐ Annual verification complete with discrepancies closed ☐ Certificates on file for every disposal ☐ Inventory reconciled to the fixed-asset ledger annually.

**N. Related.** SOP-03 · SOP-08 · SOP-09 · SOP-12 · SOP-19 · SOP-22 · Appendix F

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-12 — MOBILE DEVICE AND REMOTE WORK SECURITY

**B.** ITG-SOP-12 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Let staff work from vehicles, homes, and community settings — which this organization genuinely requires — without carrying resident and employee data into uncontrolled places.

**D. Scope.** All organization-issued mobile devices; all personal devices used for organizational work; all work performed outside organizational facilities.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] · Consulted: HR (BYOD and wage-hour implications), Compliance (PHI on mobile)

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Mobile device management ⬜ [INSERT] · VPN ⬜ [INSERT] · Appendix F · Appendix I (exceptions) · remote work agreement ⬜ [INSERT — HR-owned]

**G. Definitions.** *BYOD* — personal device used for work. *Containerization* — separating organizational data on a personal device so it can be removed independently. *Remote wipe* — remotely deleting organizational data from a device.

**H. Roles.** Employee: comply with device requirements, report loss immediately · Supervisor: confirm remote work is appropriate for the role and approved · IT: enforce technical requirements, wipe on loss or separation · HR: own the remote work agreement and any wage-hour implications.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Requirements for every device accessing organizational data** (issued or personal): a passcode or biometric lock; automatic lock within ⬜ [recommend 5 minutes]; device encryption; a currently supported operating system with current updates; enrollment in the management platform for organizational data; no jailbreaking or rooting; ability for the organization to remove organizational data remotely.
2. **Approve BYOD** individually via Appendix I or the remote work agreement, with the employee's written acknowledgment of remote-removal rights. ⬜ **[HR AND LEGAL REVIEW REQUIRED.]**
3. **Do not** store organizational files locally on a mobile device; work from [Approved File-Sharing Platform].
4. **Do not** access clinical, financial, payroll, or HR systems from a personal device unless specifically approved for that system.
5. **Never** photograph an individual served, a document containing PHI, or a screen containing confidential information on a personal device.
6. **Remote work setup:** use a private area where screens and calls are not visible or audible to others; lock the screen when stepping away; use a headset for confidential calls; never let household members use an organizational device; use a privacy screen where needed; secure any printed material and return it to the office for shredding.
7. **Connectivity:** prefer a home network with a changed default router password over public Wi-Fi; prefer a cellular hotspot over public Wi-Fi; use the approved VPN for any access to internal systems from an untrusted network; never ignore a certificate warning.
8. **In vehicles:** never leave a device visible; never leave a device in a vehicle overnight; never leave printed resident information in a vehicle; use hands-free and never work on a device while driving.
9. **Organization-issued phones** used as a site's primary contact number are life-safety equipment: keep them charged, in service, and with the assigned staff member; report failures as P1/P2 per SOP-03.
10. **Lost or stolen:** report immediately per SOP-08.2.14; IT locks and wipes; Compliance assesses if data was involved; file a police report for theft.
11. **On separation:** organizational data is removed from personal devices and the removal is documented (SOP-09 I.10.5).

**J. Escalation.** Lost/stolen device → [IT Support Phone] immediately · Unencrypted or unmanaged device found accessing organizational data → IT blocks access, notifies COO · PHI found stored locally on a personal device → Compliance same day · Repeated update deferral → supervisor and IT.

**K. Documentation/retention.** BYOD approvals and acknowledgments (7 years after separation) · device compliance reports (3 years) · wipe records (7 years) · remote work agreements (HR retention).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Remote lock/wipe within 1 hour of report · Device compliance report produced monthly · Non-compliant device blocked within 1 business day of detection.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ 100% of devices accessing organizational data are managed and encrypted ☐ Zero unsupported operating systems in use ☐ BYOD approvals on file for every personal device ☐ VPN available and tested for all remote staff ☐ Monthly compliance report reviewed ☐ Remote work agreements current.

**N. Related.** SOP-07 · SOP-08 · SOP-09 · SOP-11 · SOP-14 · SOP-19

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-13 — SOFTWARE PROCUREMENT, APPROVAL, LICENSING, AND SHADOW-IT PREVENTION

**B.** ITG-SOP-13 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Ensure every application we depend on is known, contracted, secured, budgeted, and actually used — and that departments have a fast, legitimate path to get what they need so they do not go around the process.

**D. Scope.** All software, cloud services, mobile apps, browser extensions, and AI tools used for organizational work, whether paid or free, whether centrally or departmentally procured.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] · Consulted: Finance (contracting), Compliance (BAA), Legal (terms)

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Appendix G · approved software register ⬜ [INSERT] · contract repository ⬜ [INSERT] · discovery tooling ⬜ [INSERT]

**G. Definitions.** *Shadow IT* — any application used for work that IT has not approved or inventoried. *TCO* — total cost of ownership over the expected life. *Exit cost* — what it costs to leave, including data extraction and re-implementation.

**H. Roles.** Requester: submit Appendix G with the business outcome · Department director: approve the business case · IT: assess security, integration, overlap, and TCO · Finance: contract and budget · Compliance: BAA and privacy · COO: approve above threshold.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Submit** Appendix G. State the **decision the software enables, at what cadence, for whom** — before listing features.
2. **Screen for overlap** (IT, 5 business days). **If** an existing platform already does this, **then** the request is redirected. At our scale the most common finding is that we already own the capability and nobody configured it.
3. **Assess:** security posture, data classification, BAA need, integration surface, authentication (SSO/MFA), support model, vendor stability, accessibility (SOP-24), 3-year TCO, exit cost, and data portability.
4. **Approve** per authority: no data/no cost → Director of IT · confidential data or under ⬜ [INSERT $] → Director of IT + COO · PHI, core integration, or over ⬜ [INSERT $] → COO + Finance + Compliance · above ⬜ [INSERT $] or a 5+ year commitment → CEO, and Board where policy requires.
5. **Contract** through [Director of Finance]; never allow a department to accept click-through terms for an organizational service — click-through acceptance binds the organization and almost never includes a BAA.
6. **Execute a BAA** before any PHI touches the service. No BAA, no PHI, no exceptions.
7. **Register** the application: owner, users, cost, renewal date, data classification, BAA status, admin contacts, support path, and exit plan.
8. **Deploy** with SSO and MFA where supported; provision through SOP-09.
9. **Reconcile licenses quarterly:** assigned licenses vs. active users vs. actual usage. **Report unused-license cost to Finance** — this is usually the fastest technology saving available in an organization of this size.
10. **Renew deliberately:** review 90 days before renewal against usage and need. Auto-renewal without review is how cost accumulates invisibly.
11. **Discover shadow IT quarterly:** review expense reports and credit card statements for software charges, review OAuth applications connected to Google Workspace, and survey departments. **Treat a discovery as a process failure to fix, not an employee to punish** — if someone bought a tool, they had a need the process did not meet.
12. **Remediate** discovered shadow IT: assess data exposure with Compliance; either approve it properly or migrate off it and delete the data, with confirmation.

**J. Escalation.** Shadow IT holding resident or employee data → Compliance + COO within 1 business day · Software purchased outside the process → Finance + COO · Vendor refuses a BAA for a PHI tool → stop deployment, escalate to COO · Renewal within 30 days without review → Director of IT + Finance.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Requests and approvals (7 years) · contracts and BAAs (7 years after termination) · license reconciliations (3 years) · shadow-IT findings and remediation (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Initial screening 5 business days · Full assessment 20 business days · Decision within 30 days of a complete request · Quarterly license reconciliation within 15 days of quarter end.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Register complete with owner, cost, renewal, and data classification for every application ☐ BAA on file for 100% of PHI applications ☐ Zero click-through organizational agreements ☐ Quarterly reconciliation completed and unused-license cost reported ☐ Quarterly shadow-IT discovery completed ☐ No auto-renewal without documented review.

**N. Related.** SOP-07 · SOP-09 · SOP-10 · SOP-14 · SOP-16 · SOP-22 · Appendix G

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-14 — DATA CLASSIFICATION, PRIVACY, CONFIDENTIALITY, AND SECURE FILE SHARING

**B.** ITG-SOP-14 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Give staff a four-level classification they can apply in seconds, and clear handling rules for each — because most data exposure comes from people who did not know a document was sensitive, not from people who did not care.

**D. Scope.** All organizational information in any form: electronic, paper, verbal, and photographic.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] · Co-owner: [VP Compliance] (privacy determinations) · Consulted: Legal, Clinical, HR, Development

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** [Approved File-Sharing Platform] (Google Shared Drives) · Google Vault · Appendix E · SOP-21 retention schedule

**G. Definitions — the four levels.**

| Level | Definition | Examples | Handling |
|---|---|---|---|
| **PUBLIC** | Approved for public release | Website content, published annual report, press releases, public job postings | No restriction |
| **INTERNAL** | For staff, not harmful if disclosed but not for release | Internal announcements, general procedures, org charts, meeting agendas without personal data | Organizational accounts only; do not post publicly |
| **CONFIDENTIAL** | Would harm the organization or a person if disclosed | Employee names with schedules, vendor contracts, unpublished financials, donor lists, site operational detail, security documentation | Named-recipient sharing only; no public links; encrypt in transit; shred paper |
| **RESTRICTED** | Legally protected; serious harm if disclosed | All PHI, resident records, personnel files, SSNs, financial account data, credentials, legal/investigation materials, Board executive session | Minimum necessary; named recipients only; no external attachment without an approved secure method; never in a public AI tool; never on personal devices or storage; locked storage for paper; access logged |

**H. Roles.** Employee: classify before sharing, apply the handling rule · Data owner (the department head of the system of record): define classification for their domain · IT: technical controls, share reviews, DLP where available · Compliance: privacy determinations and breach assessment.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Classify at creation.** Ask: *if this appeared on the front page of a local newspaper, who would be harmed?* That answer sets the level.
2. **Label** Confidential and Restricted documents in the header or footer.
3. **Store** organizational records in Shared Drives owned by the organization — **never solely in an individual's My Drive**, which is lost or orphaned at separation.
4. **Share with named recipients** at the minimum permission (viewer before commenter before editor). Use "anyone with the link" only for Public content.
5. **Set expiration** on external shares where supported; default ⬜ [recommend 30 days] for Confidential.
6. **Review external shares quarterly:** IT produces a report of externally shared Confidential and Restricted content; data owners certify or revoke. ⬜ **[This review is usually the single highest-yield privacy finding in a Google Workspace environment — accumulated external shares nobody remembers creating.]**
7. **Transmit Restricted data externally** only by the approved method (SOP-01 I.5.5), never as an ordinary email attachment.
8. **Apply minimum necessary** to every disclosure of PHI — the smallest amount of information needed for the purpose.
9. **Protect verbal information:** do not discuss residents or employees in public areas, vehicles with others present, or on speakerphone where others can hear.
10. **Protect paper:** locked storage for Confidential and Restricted; never left on desks overnight, in vehicles, or in home offices beyond the task; shred in locked containers.
11. **Photographs and video of individuals served** require program authorization and consent per program policy; never on personal devices; never posted to social media without the documented consent process (SOP-23).
12. **Report any suspected exposure within 1 hour** per SOP-08.

**J. Escalation.** Suspected exposure of Restricted data → IT + Compliance within 1 hour · Restricted data found in an unapproved location → IT removes, Compliance assesses · External share of Restricted data discovered → revoke immediately, assess, report · Repeated misclassification in a department → director + COO for targeted training.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Classification decisions for major data sets (current + 7 years) · quarterly external-share reviews (3 years) · exposure incident reports (6 years) · consent records for photo/video (per program policy).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* External share review completed within 30 days of quarter end · Revocation of an improper share within 4 business hours of discovery · Classification guidance response to staff within 1 business day.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Four-level standard published and trained ☐ Shared Drives are the default storage, verified by sampling ☐ Quarterly external-share review completed and certified ☐ No Restricted data in public-link shares ☐ Locked shredding available at every site ☐ Approved secure transmission method selected and in use.

**N. Related.** SOP-01 · SOP-08 · SOP-10 · SOP-12 · SOP-19 · SOP-21 · HIPAA Privacy Policy

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-15 — BACKUP, DISASTER RECOVERY, AND BUSINESS CONTINUITY COORDINATION

**B.** ITG-SOP-15 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Ensure that any system, file, or mailbox can be restored to a known-good state within a defined time, and that the organization has proven it — because an untested backup is a belief, not a control.

**D. Scope.** All organizational data and systems, including cloud platforms. **Cloud does not mean backed up:** Google Workspace protects against its own infrastructure failure, not against a user or attacker deleting or encrypting our data.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] · Executed by: [IT Provider Name] · Consulted: Finance, Clinical, Compliance

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Backup platform ⬜ [INSERT] · Google Vault · restore test log ⬜ [INSERT] · DR plan ⬜ [INSERT — separate technical document] · SOP-25 continuity procedures

**G. Definitions.** *RPO (Recovery Point Objective)* — how much data we can afford to lose, measured in time. *RTO (Recovery Time Objective)* — how long we can afford to be without the system. *Immutable backup* — a backup that cannot be altered or deleted, including by an attacker with administrative credentials. *3-2-1* — three copies, two media types, one off-site.

**H. Roles.** IT: define requirements, verify execution, run tests · MSP: execute backups, monitor, report, restore · System owners: define RPO/RTO for their systems · COO: approve RPO/RTO and the associated cost · Finance: fund; verify continuity for payroll and billing.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Define RPO and RTO for every critical system** with its owner. ⬜ **[BUILD REQUIRED — proposed starting points below, requiring leadership approval:]**

| System | Proposed RPO | Proposed RTO | Owner |
|---|---|---|---|
| Clinical/EHR documentation | 1 hour | 4 hours | Clinical |
| Medicaid billing | 4 hours | 8 hours | Billing/Finance |
| Payroll / timekeeping | 4 hours | 8 hours | HR/Finance |
| Google Workspace (email, Drive) | 4 hours | 8 hours | IT |
| Accounting / GL | 24 hours | 24 hours | Finance |
| Work order system | 24 hours | 24 hours | IT |
| Donor CRM | 24 hours | 48 hours | Development |
| Phone system | N/A | 4 hours | IT |

2. **Back up** on a schedule that meets each RPO; include Google Workspace with a third-party backup ⬜ [INSERT DECISION — required to meet the RPO above].
3. **Maintain at least one immutable, off-site copy** — this is the specific control that survives ransomware.
4. **Monitor** backup jobs daily; **if** a job fails, **then** it is remediated within 1 business day and reported if it fails twice.
5. **Test restores** ⬜ [recommend quarterly]: restore a sample file, a full mailbox, and one full system; document what was restored, how long it took, and whether it was usable. **A backup that has not been restored has not been tested.**
6. **Conduct** an annual disaster recovery exercise simulating loss of a primary system, with a written after-action report.
7. **Coordinate** with SOP-25: technical recovery restores systems; SOP-25 keeps services running while they are down. Both are needed.
8. **Verify** backups after any major change (migration, platform change, new application).
9. **Report** monthly: job success rate, failures, restore tests performed, RPO/RTO compliance.
10. **Confirm** cyber-insurance conditions related to backup are met and documented.

**J. Escalation.** Backup failure unresolved 48 hours → Director of IT → COO · Restore test fails → COO immediately; treat as a critical risk · RPO/RTO cannot be met with current investment → COO for a funding decision, documented as accepted risk until resolved · Any backup deleted or altered unexpectedly → treat as a security incident (SOP-08).

**K. Documentation/retention.** Backup configuration and schedule (current + 3 years) · daily job logs (1 year) · restore test records (3 years) · DR exercise reports (7 years) · RPO/RTO approvals (current + 7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Backup success rate ≥99% monthly · Restore test completed quarterly at 100% · Failed job remediated within 1 business day · Single-file restore within 4 business hours · Mailbox restore within 1 business day · Full system restore within the approved RTO.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ RPO/RTO defined and approved for every critical system ☐ Google Workspace backed up independently ☐ At least one immutable off-site copy verified ☐ Quarterly restore tests completed and documented ☐ Annual DR exercise completed ☐ Monthly reporting delivered ☐ Insurance backup conditions met.

**N. Related.** SOP-06 · SOP-08 · SOP-16 · SOP-17 · SOP-25

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-16 — VENDOR AND MANAGED SERVICE PROVIDER MANAGEMENT

**B.** ITG-SOP-16 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Manage technology vendors — above all the MSP — on measured performance rather than impression, and ensure every vendor touching our data is contractually bound to protect it.

**D. Scope.** All technology vendors, the managed service provider, cloud application vendors, ISPs, the VoIP provider, the print vendor, and any vendor with access to our systems, data, or facilities.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] · Co-owner: [Director of Finance] (contracting, spend) · Consulted: Compliance (BAA), Legal (terms)

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Vendor register ⬜ [INSERT] · contract repository ⬜ [INSERT] · scorecard template (below) · Appendix G · Appendix H

**G. Definitions.** *Tier 1 vendor* — failure stops operations or exposes data (MSP, EHR, billing, payroll, ISP, VoIP). *Tier 2* — significant disruption. *Tier 3* — inconvenience. *BAA* — Business Associate Agreement.

**H. Roles.** Director of IT: technical performance, escalation, scorecards · Finance: contracts, spend, renewals, remedies · Compliance: BAA and privacy terms · COO: relationship ownership for Tier 1, renewal and replacement decisions · Legal: terms and disputes.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Register** every vendor: name, tier, services, contract dates, renewal and notice dates, annual cost, BAA status, security assurance held, primary and escalation contacts, and the internal owner.
2. **Require before any access to systems or data:** executed contract; BAA where PHI is involved; named individuals with time-boxed access (SOP-09 I.4); MFA; the approved remote-access method; documented breach-notification obligations; insurance certificates; and background screening where the vendor enters residential sites.
3. **Define MSP service levels in the contract** and reconcile them to this manual's targets. ⬜ **[REQUIRED BEFORE ADOPTION: the internal SLAs proposed in SOP-03, SOP-05, and SOP-06 must be supported by the MSP agreement, or they are promises the organization cannot keep. Where a gap exists, either renegotiate or publish the achievable number.]**
4. **Score Tier 1 vendors quarterly:**

| Measure | Weight | Source |
|---|---|---|
| First-response SLA attainment | 20% | Ticket system |
| Resolution SLA attainment | 20% | Ticket system |
| Tickets reopened / repeat issues | 10% | Ticket system |
| Proactive detection (issues found before reported) | 10% | MSP report |
| Patch and update compliance | 10% | MSP report |
| Backup success and restore tests | 10% | SOP-15 |
| Communication quality and escalation responsiveness | 10% | IT and site feedback |
| Invoice accuracy and change-order discipline | 10% | Finance |

5. **Meet** with the MSP monthly (operational) and quarterly (business review with COO and Finance present).
6. **Escalate** documented performance failures in writing, with a remediation plan, dates, and a consequence if unmet.
7. **Review renewals 90 days out** against the scorecard, current market pricing, switching cost, and data portability. Never renew on autopilot.
8. **Assess switching cost before it is needed** for every Tier 1 vendor: data extraction, re-implementation, retraining, parallel running, and contract exit terms. **A vendor whose exit cost is unknown holds more leverage than their contract gives them.**
9. **Review vendor security annually:** assurance reports, breach history, subprocessor changes, and any material change in ownership or terms.
10. **Offboard vendors** completely: disable access, recover data, obtain a deletion certificate, remove from the register, and confirm no remaining named-contact authority at banks, carriers, or platforms.

**J. Escalation.** Tier 1 vendor misses SLA two consecutive months → COO + Finance, formal remediation · Vendor security incident affecting our data → SOP-08 immediately, Compliance and Legal · Vendor refuses a BAA → stop the engagement for PHI use · Vendor access found active after engagement end → treat as a security finding, COO notified · Unbudgeted vendor cost increase above ⬜ [INSERT %] → Finance + COO.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Contracts and BAAs (7 years after termination) · scorecards (7 years) · meeting minutes and escalations (7 years) · vendor security assessments (7 years) · offboarding and deletion certificates (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Quarterly scorecards within 15 days of quarter end · Renewal review at 90 days, 100% · Vendor access removed within 1 business day of engagement end · BAA executed before any PHI access, 100%.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Vendor register complete with tier, cost, renewal, BAA, and owner ☐ MSP SLAs reconciled to this manual ☐ Quarterly scorecards produced for all Tier 1 ☐ Switching cost documented for all Tier 1 ☐ Zero vendors with PHI access lacking a BAA ☐ No auto-renewals without review ☐ Zero active vendor accounts past engagement end.

**N. Related.** SOP-03 · SOP-08 · SOP-09 · SOP-13 · SOP-15 · SOP-22 · Appendix G, H

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-17 — INCIDENT MANAGEMENT, MAJOR-INCIDENT COMMUNICATIONS, AND POST-INCIDENT REVIEW

**B.** ITG-SOP-17 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Run any significant disruption — security, outage, or system failure — through one command structure with one communication channel, so that the organization responds once instead of in six directions.

**D. Scope.** All incidents affecting technology, communications, or data, including security incidents (SOP-08), outages (SOP-06, SOP-25), and critical system failures.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] (Incident Commander for technical response) · Consulted: all executives per severity

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Appendix E · Appendix N · incident log ⬜ [INSERT] · mass-notification channel ⬜ [INSERT] · conference bridge ⬜ [INSERT] · out-of-band communication method ⬜ **[REQUIRED — if email and phones are the incident, the response cannot run on email and phones. Recommend a pre-agreed alternate: a group SMS list plus a designated cell bridge.]**

**G. Definitions.** *Major incident* — SEV 1 or SEV 2 (SOP-08 I.7), or any event affecting multiple sites, resident safety, or a regulatory obligation. *Incident Commander* — the single person coordinating technical response. *Business Lead* — the single person coordinating operational impact and communications.

**H. Roles.**
| Role | Held by | Responsibility |
|---|---|---|
| Incident Commander | [Director of Information Technology] or designee | Technical response, resource coordination, technical decisions |
| Business Lead | [Chief Operating Officer] or designee | Operational continuity, communications, executive decisions |
| Scribe | Assigned at declaration | Timeline, decisions, and actions logged contemporaneously |
| Communications Lead | [Marketing/Communications Contact] | External messaging, drafted from Appendix N, approved by CEO/Legal |
| Function Leads | VP Ops, Clinical, Finance, HR, Compliance | Continuity within their function |

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Detect and report** — anyone may report; IT triages within 30 minutes.
2. **Assign severity** (SOP-08 I.7). When uncertain, assign the higher severity; downgrading later is cheap, upgrading late is not.
3. **Declare a major incident** for SEV 1–2: [Chief Operating Officer] declares, or [Director of Information Technology] declares and notifies the COO immediately.
4. **Activate** the roles above and open the single communication channel. **All updates flow through that channel; no parallel side-channel updates.**
5. **Stabilize first:** protect people, then contain damage, then restore service, then investigate. Investigation never delays containment.
6. **Communicate on a fixed cadence** — every 30 minutes for SEV 1, every 2 hours for SEV 2 — even when the update is "no change." Silence during an incident generates rumor, duplicate reporting, and staff improvising workarounds that create new problems.
7. **Use Appendix N templates.** External communication requires [Executive Director/CEO] and [Legal Counsel Contact] approval.
8. **Maintain the timeline contemporaneously.** Reconstructing it afterward produces an unreliable record and weakens both the insurance claim and any regulatory response.
9. **Stand down** when service is verified restored and contained: Business Lead confirms operations with affected sites, Incident Commander confirms technical closure, and a stand-down notice goes out on the same channel.
10. **Review within 10 business days** using SOP-08 I.10. Assign corrective actions with owners and dates.
11. **Track corrective actions** to completion at the Technology Governance Committee; report overdue items in the dashboard.
12. **Report** SEV 1 incidents to the CEO and Board Chair; annually report incident trends to the Board.

**J. Escalation.** Any SEV 1 → CEO within 1 hour · Incident exceeding the expected duration by 2× → COO reassesses resourcing and considers external assistance · Any regulatory notification obligation → Compliance and Legal immediately · Any media contact → Communications Lead and CEO, never the site.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Incident records with timeline (7 years) · communications issued (7 years) · post-incident reviews (7 years) · corrective action tracking (7 years) · SEV 1 Board reports (permanent).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Triage 30 minutes · Major incident declared within 1 hour of severity assignment · First communication within 1 hour of declaration · Updates on cadence, 100% · Post-incident review within 10 business days · Corrective actions closed within 60 days.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Roles named with designated backups ☐ Out-of-band communication method selected and tested ☐ Appendix N templates pre-approved by Legal ☐ Contemporaneous timeline kept for every major incident ☐ Annual tabletop completed ☐ No overdue corrective actions beyond 60 days.

**N. Related.** SOP-06 · SOP-08 · SOP-15 · SOP-25 · Appendix E, N

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-18 — CHANGE MANAGEMENT FOR SIGNIFICANT TECHNOLOGY CHANGES

**B.** ITG-SOP-18 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Prevent self-inflicted outages. In organizations of this size, more downtime comes from unplanned or uncommunicated changes than from attackers.

**D. Scope.** All changes to systems, applications, network, telephony, access configuration, security controls, and integrations — including changes made by the MSP and by application vendors.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology]

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Change log ⬜ [INSERT] · change request form ⬜ [INSERT or use the work order system] · maintenance calendar ⬜ [INSERT]

**G. Definitions.** *Standard change* — pre-approved, low-risk, routine (a documented, repeatable task). *Normal change* — requires review and approval. *Emergency change* — required to restore service or address an active threat. *Rollback plan* — the documented steps to return to the prior state.

**H. Roles.** Requester: submit with business justification · Director of IT: assess risk, approve normal changes · COO: approve high-risk and cross-functional changes · System owner: approve changes to their system · MSP: submit changes through this process, not around it.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Classify** the change as standard, normal, or emergency.
2. **Submit** a request containing: what is changing, why, who is affected, when, expected downtime, risk, testing performed, rollback plan, and communication plan.
3. **Assess risk:** which sites, whether residential programs or clinical systems are affected, whether it touches security controls, and whether it can be reversed.
4. **Approve:**

| Change risk | Approver |
|---|---|
| Standard (pre-approved list) | Technician; logged |
| Normal, single system, no resident-facing impact | [Director of Information Technology] |
| Normal, multi-site or affecting clinical/financial systems | [Director of Information Technology] + system owner |
| High risk, security controls, core platform, or resident-service impact | [Director of Information Technology] + [Chief Operating Officer] |
| Platform migration or 5+ year commitment | COO + CEO (+ Board where policy requires) |
| Emergency | Director of IT verbally, documented within 1 business day, reviewed at the next committee |

5. **Schedule** in the maintenance window ⬜ **[INSERT — recommend a fixed weekly window. Note that this organization has no true "off hours" at residential sites; the window should be chosen for the lowest clinical documentation and medication administration activity, in consultation with Clinical and Operations.]**
6. **Communicate** in advance: ⬜ [recommend 5 business days for normal changes, 24 hours minimum] to all affected staff and sites, stating what changes, when, what to expect, and who to call.
7. **Test** before production wherever a test path exists.
8. **Execute** with the rollback plan ready and a named person able to execute it.
9. **Verify** after the change with a functional test, not just a status check — and confirm with a user at an affected site.
10. **Log** the change: what, when, by whom, approved by whom, outcome, and issues.
11. **Review** failed changes at the Technology Governance Committee; a change that caused an outage gets the SOP-17 post-incident treatment.
12. **Apply this SOP to vendor-initiated changes:** require advance notice from Tier 1 vendors in the contract, and log vendor-side changes that affect us. ⬜ **[Vendor-initiated changes — an EHR update, a Google feature rollout, an MSP tooling change — cause a meaningful share of surprise disruptions. Contract for notice.]**

**J. Escalation.** Change causes an outage → SOP-17 immediately · Change made outside this process → Director of IT → COO; repeated occurrence is a performance matter (or an MSP contract matter) · Emergency change not documented within 1 business day → COO · Vendor change without notice → vendor escalation and scorecard impact.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Change requests and approvals (7 years) · change log (7 years) · rollback and verification records (3 years) · failed-change reviews (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Normal change assessed within 5 business days · Advance notice ≥5 business days (24 hours minimum) · Emergency change documented within 1 business day · Change success rate ≥95% · Zero unlogged changes.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Standard change list defined and current ☐ Maintenance window agreed with Clinical and Operations ☐ Every change logged with approver ☐ Rollback plan documented for every normal and high-risk change ☐ Post-change verification performed with a real user ☐ Vendor notice requirements in Tier 1 contracts.

**N. Related.** SOP-06 · SOP-15 · SOP-16 · SOP-17 · SOP-25

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.
