# SOP-19 — PHYSICAL SECURITY FOR TECHNOLOGY ASSETS AND SPACES

**B.** ITG-SOP-19 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Protect devices, printed information, network equipment, and access credentials at sites that are people's homes — where standard office physical-security assumptions do not apply.

**D. Scope.** All sites: offices, group homes, supervised apartments, day programs, the ICF, maintenance and fleet facilities, and any location where organizational equipment or records are kept.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Facilities & Fleet Management] (physical spaces, keys, badges) and [Director of Information Technology] (equipment, closets, media) · Consulted: VP of Operations, Compliance

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Key and badge register ⬜ [INSERT] · access control system ⬜ [INSERT] · asset inventory (SOP-11) · Appendix D (offboarding recovery)

**G. Definitions.** *Restricted space* — server/network closet, IT storage, records storage, medication room, finance office. *Tailgating* — following an authorized person through a secured door. *Clean desk* — no confidential material left visible when unattended.

**H. Roles.** Employee: lock screens, secure devices and paper, challenge or report unknown visitors · Supervisor: control site keys and access, verify contractors, maintain the clean-desk standard · Facilities: key and badge issuance and recovery, locks, cameras, alarm systems · IT: network closets, equipment security, media storage.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Network and server closets:** locked at all times; access limited to named IT and Facilities staff and the MSP; access logged; no storage of unrelated materials; no resident or staff access. ⬜ **[ASSESSMENT REQUIRED — in residential settings network equipment is often in a utility area, a basement, or a closet also used for other purposes. Identify every location and secure it or document a compensating control.]**
2. **Devices:** locked screens when unattended; laptops secured or removed at end of day; shared program computers positioned so screens are not visible to visitors or individuals served; cable locks in higher-traffic areas.
3. **Printers and MFDs:** placed where output is not visible to visitors or individuals served; confidential printing per SOP-04 I.3; devices not left with output in the tray.
4. **Paper:** clean-desk standard; locked storage for Confidential and Restricted; locked shredding containers at every site; records never left in vehicles, common areas, or unattended offices.
5. **Keys:** issued from a register with the holder's name, date, and site; never copied; returned at separation or role change; **if** a key is lost, **then** report immediately and assess rekeying — cost of rekeying is a Facilities budget item, not a reason to delay.
6. **Badges and access cards:** issued individually, never shared, never loaned; deactivated at separation per SOP-09 I.9; reviewed quarterly against the employee roster.
7. **Visitors and contractors:** sign in; verify identity and the expected appointment with Facilities or the supervisor; escort in restricted spaces; never admit an unscheduled vendor; never allow a contractor to connect equipment to the network without IT approval.
8. **Tailgating:** do not hold a secured door for someone you do not recognize; direct them to the front desk or the supervisor. This is not rudeness; it is the control.
9. **Media:** storage media, backup media, and decommissioned drives stored in locked storage until destruction; never in an open area, never in a vehicle.
10. **Cameras and alarm systems:** owned by Facilities; camera placement in residential settings requires program and Compliance approval and must respect residents' privacy rights. ⬜ **[COMPLIANCE AND LEGAL REVIEW — camera use in licensed residential settings is regulated and touches residents' rights.]**
11. **Report** any physical security concern through [Work Order Submission Link] (category `Security – Doors/Locks/Badges/Cameras`) or immediately by phone if a space is left unsecured.

**J. Escalation.** Site left unsecured → supervisor + Facilities immediately; on-call manager for residential · Lost key or badge → Facilities same day; rekeying decision within 5 business days · Unauthorized person in a restricted space → supervisor + Facilities + IT; treat as a security incident · Network closet found unlocked or shared → IT + COO · Device stolen from a site → SOP-08 + police report.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Key and badge register (current + 7 years) · visitor logs (⬜ 3 years) · quarterly badge reviews (3 years) · physical security incidents (7 years) · rekeying records (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Badge deactivation at separation per SOP-09 timing · Lost key assessed within 1 business day · Unsecured space remedied within 4 hours (immediately at residential sites) · Quarterly badge review within 15 days of quarter end.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Every network/equipment location identified and secured ☐ Key and badge registers reconciled to the roster quarterly ☐ Locked shredding at every site ☐ Clean-desk standard observed (spot-checked) ☐ Visitor and contractor verification practiced ☐ No shared badges ☐ Camera placements approved by Compliance.

**N. Related.** SOP-04 · SOP-08 · SOP-09 · SOP-11 · SOP-14 · Appendix D

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-20 — SECURITY AWARENESS TRAINING AND PHISHING SIMULATION

**B.** ITG-SOP-20 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Build and measure the human layer of defense, which is the layer attackers actually target — and do it in a way that increases reporting rather than driving mistakes underground.

**D. Scope.** All users, including part-time and per-diem staff, contractors with accounts, and Board members.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] · Co-owner: [Chief Human Resources Officer] (assignment, completion tracking, records)

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Training platform ⬜ [INSERT] · phishing simulation platform ⬜ [INSERT] · LMS completion reporting · SOP-08 I.11 outline

**G. Definitions.** *Simulation* — a safe, organization-run fake phishing message used for practice. *Click rate* — the percentage of recipients who clicked. *Report rate* — the percentage who reported it. *Repeat clicker* — someone who clicks in multiple consecutive simulations.

**H. Roles.** Employee: complete training, report simulations like real messages · Supervisor: drive completion, coach without shaming · IT: run the program, analyze results, tailor content · HR: assign, track, retain records · COO: review results, approve consequences policy.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Assign** orientation training before account activation and annual training within 30 days of assignment.
2. **Run phishing simulations** ⬜ [recommend monthly for higher-risk roles, quarterly for all staff].
3. **Design simulations around real risk:** vendor banking changes, executive impersonation, payroll diversion, regulator-themed attachments, shared-drive link lures. Use scenarios that mirror SOP-08 I.12.
4. **Measure both click rate and report rate.** **Report rate is the more important number** — an organization where people click occasionally but report immediately is far safer than one where nobody clicks and nobody reports.
5. **Respond to a click with immediate education**, not discipline: a short just-in-time module explaining what the cues were.
6. **Manage repeat clickers** supportively: after ⬜ [recommend 3 clicks in 12 months], the supervisor and IT conduct a coaching conversation, assign targeted training, and consider whether the person's role, workload, or workstation environment is contributing. **Consider discipline only for refusal to complete required training, not for clicking.** ⬜ **[HR REVIEW REQUIRED on any consequence framework.]**
7. **Never** design a simulation around emotionally exploitative themes — bonuses, layoffs, benefits changes, or family emergencies. They work, and they destroy trust and reporting, which costs more than they teach.
8. **Tailor role-specific content** per SOP-08 I.11.
9. **Report** monthly: completion by department, click rate, report rate, median report time, repeat clickers (in aggregate, not by name, for general reporting).
10. **Feed** real incidents back into training within ⬜ [recommend 30 days] — the most effective training material is what actually happened here.
11. **Accommodate** staff who need alternate formats, translation, or assistive technology (SOP-24). Training that a staff member cannot access is a control that does not exist for them.

**J. Escalation.** Department completion below ⬜ [recommend 90%] at 45 days → director → COO · Organization-wide click rate above ⬜ [recommend 10%] → COO; program redesign · Report rate below ⬜ [recommend 40%] → COO; investigate whether reporting is too hard or staff fear consequences · Refusal to complete training → HR.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Training completion (7 years after separation) · simulation campaign results (3 years) · coaching records (HR retention) · curriculum versions (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Annual completion ≥95% · New hire training before account activation, 100% · Simulation cadence maintained · Median report time ≤ ⬜ [recommend 30 minutes] · Monthly reporting delivered within 10 days of month end.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Every active user assigned current training ☐ Completion tracked in one system ☐ Simulations run on schedule ☐ Report rate trending up ☐ Real incidents incorporated within 30 days ☐ No exploitative simulation themes used ☐ Accessible formats available.

**N. Related.** SOP-08 · SOP-09 · SOP-24 · Section 9 · Appendix J

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-21 — RECORDS RETENTION AND LEGAL HOLD FOR ELECTRONIC COMMUNICATIONS AND FILES

**B.** ITG-SOP-21 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Keep what we are required to keep, dispose of what we are permitted to dispose of, and stop all disposal instantly when litigation, investigation, audit, or survey is anticipated.

**D. Scope.** All electronic records: email, files, chat, work orders, system logs, voicemail, and application data; and printed records generated from organizational systems.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] (technical execution) · **Authority owner:** [VP Compliance] and [Legal Counsel Contact] (retention periods and hold decisions)

⬜ **[LEGAL AND COMPLIANCE REVIEW REQUIRED. Retention periods stated anywhere in this manual are proposals only. Legal Counsel must confirm each period against HIPAA, CMS, DDD, Medicaid, employment, tax, grant, donor, and contractual requirements before adoption.]**

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Google Vault · [Data Retention Policy] ⬜ [INSERT — the authoritative schedule] · legal hold notice template (Appendix L) · hold register ⬜ [INSERT]

**G. Definitions.** *Record* — information documenting a decision, transaction, service, or obligation, regardless of format. *Transitory record* — routine material with no lasting value. *Legal hold* — a directive suspending disposal. *Spoliation* — destruction of evidence, which carries serious legal consequences and can be inferred against the organization.

**H. Roles.** Employee: retain records, stop deletion on notice, never delete anything related to a known matter · Supervisor: ensure staff comply with holds · IT: apply technical retention and holds, produce records on request · Compliance/Legal: set periods, issue and release holds, direct production.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Maintain one authoritative retention schedule** — the [Data Retention Policy] — covering every record type and system. The summary in Section 8.3 is a convenience copy and never overrides it.
2. **Configure** Vault retention rules to match the schedule, and verify annually.
3. **Retain** records in the system of record, not in individual mailboxes and personal drives.
4. **Dispose** only per the schedule, only with no hold in effect, and only through a documented process; log disposals by record category and date range.
5. **Issue a legal hold** when litigation, investigation, audit, survey, or a regulatory inquiry is reasonably anticipated — **not when it is filed.** The obligation begins at anticipation.
   a. [Legal Counsel Contact] or [VP Compliance] issues a written notice identifying custodians, subject matter, date range, and record types.
   b. Custodians acknowledge receipt in writing.
   c. IT applies a technical hold in Vault and in affected applications, and confirms in writing within 1 business day.
   d. IT suspends all automated deletion within the hold scope.
   e. IT preserves departing custodians' mailboxes and files regardless of the normal offboarding schedule.
6. **Maintain a hold register:** matter, issuer, date, custodians, scope, systems, status, and release date.
7. **Remind** custodians of active holds ⬜ [recommend quarterly].
8. **Release** holds only in writing by the issuer; resume normal retention afterward and document it.
9. **Produce records** only through [Legal Counsel Contact] and [VP Compliance]. Staff never respond directly to a subpoena, records request, or attorney letter (SOP-01 I.6).
10. **Never delete anything** related to an incident, complaint, investigation, or regulatory matter — and report any instruction to do so (SOP-01 I.8.7).
11. **Verify** holds are correctly applied during the annual review; a hold that was issued but never technically applied is a serious exposure.

**J. Escalation.** Hold not applied within 1 business day → COO + Legal · Records deleted within a hold scope → Legal immediately; preserve evidence of what happened · Instruction to delete records → Compliance + Legal same day · Retention configuration does not match the schedule → COO.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Retention schedule (permanent, with version history) · hold notices, acknowledgments, and releases (permanent) · hold register (permanent) · disposal logs (7 years) · production records (per matter).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Hold applied within 1 business day of issuance · Custodian acknowledgment within 3 business days · Record production per Legal's deadline · Annual retention configuration verification completed.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ One authoritative retention schedule, legally reviewed ☐ Vault rules match the schedule, verified annually ☐ Hold register current ☐ Every active hold technically verified ☐ Departing custodians under hold preserved ☐ Disposal logged.

**N. Related.** SOP-01 · SOP-08 · SOP-09 · SOP-14 · Section 8.3, 8.4

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-22 — TECHNOLOGY BUDGET, LIFECYCLE REPLACEMENT, AND ASSET REFRESH PLANNING

**B.** ITG-SOP-22 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Convert technology spending from unpredictable emergency replacement into a planned, funded, multi-year schedule — which is both cheaper and less disruptive.

**D. Scope.** All technology operating and capital spending: devices, infrastructure, software licenses, cloud subscriptions, MSP fees, telecommunications, AI tools, and project costs.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] and [Director of Finance]

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Asset inventory (SOP-11) · software register (SOP-13) · vendor register (SOP-16) · budget model ⬜ [INSERT] · capital request process ⬜ [INSERT]

**G. Definitions.** *Lifecycle* — the planned service life of an asset class. *Refresh* — planned replacement at end of life. *TCO* — total cost of ownership. *Technical debt* — deferred replacement and upgrades that accumulate as risk and cost.

**H. Roles.** Director of IT: build and maintain the lifecycle plan, forecast spend · Director of Finance: budget integration, capitalization, funding source · COO: approve the plan and trade-offs · CEO/Board: capital approval per policy.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Set lifecycle standards** ⬜ *(proposed, requiring leadership approval)*:

| Asset class | Proposed life | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Laptops / desktops | 4–5 years | Shared site workstations run harder; consider 4 |
| Tablets | 3–4 years | |
| Mobile phones | 3 years | Tied to carrier contract |
| Printers / MFDs | 5–7 years | Or per lease |
| Desk phones | 7 years | |
| Network equipment (switches, APs, firewalls) | 5 years | Firewalls tied to support/subscription end |
| Servers (if any on premises) | 5 years | |
| Hotspots | 3 years | |

2. **Build a rolling 5-year refresh schedule** from the asset inventory: count assets by class and purchase year, apply the lifecycle, and produce the annual replacement volume and cost.
3. **Smooth the curve.** **If** a single year carries a disproportionate replacement load, **then** shift purchases forward or back a year to level the budget — a large one-year spike is usually the residue of a past bulk purchase and will otherwise repeat forever.
4. **Budget annually** across four categories: (a) recurring operating — MSP, licenses, connectivity, cloud; (b) lifecycle replacement; (c) projects and improvements; (d) contingency ⬜ [recommend 5–10% of the technology operating budget].
5. **Track actual versus budget monthly** and report variance to Finance and the COO.
6. **Report unused-license and unused-subscription cost quarterly** (SOP-13) and reallocate the savings inside the technology budget rather than surrendering them — this funds improvement without new money.
7. **Evaluate every material purchase on:** integration surface, total cost of ownership, exit cost, and data portability — **before** features. At this scale a platform decision is a five-to-ten-year commitment, and the exit cost is usually larger than the purchase price.
8. **Fund replacement before failure.** Report annually the number and percentage of devices past their lifecycle; **if** that figure rises year over year, **then** the organization is accruing technical debt and the dashboard should say so plainly.
9. **Tie recurring-issue decisions** (SOP-03 I.9) to the refresh plan — a device generating repeated tickets is a replacement candidate with a documented cost justification already attached.
10. **Coordinate** with Facilities and Fleet capital planning; the shared asset data model means technology, building systems, and vehicles can be forecast in one capital view.
11. **Model grant and funding opportunities** for technology that supports service quality, accessibility, or efficiency, in coordination with [Director of Development].

**J. Escalation.** Devices past lifecycle exceeding ⬜ [recommend 15%] of the fleet → COO · Unbudgeted emergency replacement above ⬜ [INSERT $] → COO + Finance · Budget variance above ⬜ [recommend 10%] → Finance + COO · A platform decision with an undocumented exit cost → COO, decision paused until documented.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Lifecycle plan and annual updates (7 years) · budget submissions and variance reports (7 years) · TCO and exit-cost analyses for major decisions (7 years) · capital approvals (permanent).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* 5-year plan updated annually before budget season · Monthly variance reporting within 10 days of month end · Quarterly license reconciliation · Devices past lifecycle below ⬜ [recommend 10%].

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Lifecycle standards approved ☐ Refresh schedule built from the actual inventory, not estimates ☐ Budget categories separated ☐ Variance reported monthly ☐ Unused-license cost quantified quarterly ☐ TCO and exit cost documented for every major decision ☐ Past-lifecycle percentage trending down.

**N. Related.** SOP-03 · SOP-11 · SOP-13 · SOP-16 · Part IV

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-23 — WEBSITE, SOCIAL MEDIA, AND EXTERNAL DIGITAL COMMUNICATIONS GOVERNANCE

**B.** ITG-SOP-23 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Protect the organization's public voice and the privacy and dignity of the people it serves, and ensure the website and social accounts are controlled assets rather than orphaned logins.

**D. Scope.** The organization's website, social media accounts, email marketing, donor communications, online directories, review platforms, job postings, and any digital channel representing [Organization Name].

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Marketing/Communications Contact] and [Director of Development] · Consulted: IT (technical), Compliance (privacy and consent), Legal, CEO (position statements)

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Website platform ⬜ [INSERT] · social accounts register ⬜ [INSERT] · domain registrar ⬜ **[INSERT — and confirm the organization, not an individual or a former vendor, owns the domain and the registrar account. This is a common and serious finding.]** · media consent forms ⬜ [INSERT — program-owned] · Appendix N

**G. Definitions.** *Official channel* — an account authorized to speak for the organization. *Personal channel* — an employee's own account. *Consent* — documented authorization to use a person's image, name, or story.

**H. Roles.** Marketing/Communications: content, brand, publishing, monitoring · Development: donor and philanthropic content · IT: domain, hosting, DNS, security, access control · Compliance: consent and privacy review · CEO: public positions and crisis statements · Employees: follow the personal-use standards below.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Register every official channel** with: platform, handle, purpose, owner, administrators, and recovery contacts. **No official channel may be tied to an individual's personal account or personal email** — an orphaned social account after a departure is both a brand risk and a security risk.
2. **Control access** through organizational accounts with MFA; review administrators quarterly (SOP-09); remove access immediately at separation.
3. **Protect the domain and DNS:** organizational ownership, MFA on the registrar, registry lock where available, auto-renew enabled, and expiration monitored. **A lapsed domain takes down email and the website simultaneously.**
4. **Never publish** any information about an individual served — name, image, story, or identifying detail — without documented consent obtained through the program consent process, and never where consent has been withdrawn.
5. **Review** all content referencing individuals served with Compliance and the program before publication.
6. **Approve** content per authority: routine posts → Marketing · donor and fundraising → Development + Marketing · program content referencing individuals → + Compliance and program leadership · organizational positions, advocacy, crisis, or media → CEO + Legal.
7. **Respond to public comments and reviews** per a written response standard; **never** confirm, deny, or discuss whether a person receives services, and never respond to a clinical or complaint matter publicly. Move it to a private channel and route to the appropriate leader.
8. **Handle media inquiries** per SOP-01 I.6 — always to Marketing and the CEO, never answered at a site.
9. **Employee personal use:** you may say you work here; you may not speak for the organization; never post about individuals served, coworkers' confidential matters, or organizational incidents; never post photos taken at a site; use a disclaimer when discussing the field professionally. ⬜ **[HR AND LEGAL REVIEW REQUIRED — personal social media policy must respect protected concerted activity and applicable law.]**
10. **Maintain accessibility** on the website per SOP-24 ⬜ [recommend WCAG 2.1 AA as the standard].
11. **Secure web forms** that collect personal information: encrypted transmission, controlled storage, a privacy notice, and no PHI collected through an unassessed form.
12. **Monitor** for impersonation accounts and fraudulent donation pages; report and take down. **Fraudulent donation pages impersonating nonprofits are common; monitor for them at least ⬜ [recommend quarterly].**
13. **Preserve** public communications as records per SOP-21.

**J. Escalation.** Unauthorized post from an official channel → Marketing + COO + IT immediately; treat as a possible account compromise · Individual served identified without consent → Compliance immediately; remove content; assess as a privacy incident · Domain or DNS issue → IT + COO immediately · Impersonation or fraudulent donation page → Marketing + Development + Legal · Negative public matter → CEO + Legal before any response.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Channel register and administrator reviews (current + 7 years) · consent records (per program policy) · published content archive (per SOP-21) · approval records for significant content (7 years) · takedown and impersonation reports (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Administrator review quarterly · Unauthorized content removed within 1 hour of detection · Media inquiry acknowledged within 4 business hours · Domain expiration monitored with ≥90 days' notice.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ All channels registered with organizational ownership ☐ Domain and registrar owned by the organization with MFA and auto-renew ☐ MFA on all channels ☐ Quarterly administrator review ☐ Consent on file for all published content referencing individuals served ☐ Website accessibility assessed ☐ Impersonation monitoring performed.

**N. Related.** SOP-01 · SOP-09 · SOP-10 · SOP-14 · SOP-24 · Appendix N

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-24 — TECHNOLOGY ACCESSIBILITY AND ACCOMMODATION

**B.** ITG-SOP-24 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Ensure that staff with disabilities can do their jobs, that individuals served and families can reach and use our digital services, and that accessibility is designed in at procurement rather than retrofitted after a complaint.

> **An organization whose mission is supporting people with intellectual and developmental disabilities is held — properly — to a higher standard on its own accessibility than a typical employer. Inaccessible technology here is a mission failure before it is a compliance failure.**

**D. Scope.** All organizational technology used by staff, individuals served, families, guardians, donors, job applicants, and the public: applications, devices, the website, documents, training materials, and communications.

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [Director of Information Technology] (technical) · Co-owner: [Chief Human Resources Officer] (employee accommodation process) · Consulted: Clinical, Compliance, Marketing, Legal

⬜ **[HR AND LEGAL REVIEW REQUIRED — employee accommodation is an HR-owned legal process under the ADA and the New Jersey Law Against Discrimination. This SOP addresses the technology component only and does not alter HR's process or authority.]**

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** HR accommodation request process ⬜ [INSERT] · assistive technology inventory ⬜ [INSERT] · accessibility standard ⬜ [recommend WCAG 2.1 AA] · procurement checklist (SOP-13)

**G. Definitions.** *Assistive technology* — hardware or software that enables a person with a disability to use a system (screen readers, magnification, alternative input, captioning, communication devices). *Reasonable accommodation* — a change enabling a qualified person to perform essential job functions. *Plain language* — clear, direct writing at an accessible reading level.

**H. Roles.** Employee: request accommodation through HR · HR: run the interactive accommodation process and determine the accommodation · IT: assess technical options, implement, support, and fund from the technology budget where appropriate · Procurement/IT: require accessibility in purchasing · Marketing: website and public content accessibility.

**I. Procedure.**
1. **Employee accommodation:** the employee requests through [Human Resources Contact]; HR runs the interactive process; HR engages IT for technical options, cost, and implementation timeline; IT implements the approved accommodation and supports it thereafter.
2. **Do not** require an employee to disclose a diagnosis to IT. IT receives the functional requirement from HR, not medical information.
3. **Implement promptly** — an approved accommodation delayed by procurement is a denied accommodation in practice. ⬜ [recommend 10 business days for standard assistive software or peripherals].
4. **Assess accessibility at procurement:** every new application or device is evaluated for keyboard navigation, screen reader compatibility, captioning, contrast, text scaling, and a vendor accessibility statement (VPAT or equivalent). Record the result in the software register; **if** a product fails and is still selected, **then** document the compensating accommodation plan. ⬜ **[This is the single most effective accessibility control — it costs nothing at purchase and is expensive to retrofit.]**
5. **Make training accessible:** captioned video, alternative formats, plain language, screen-reader-compatible materials, translation where needed (SOP-20 I.11).
6. **Make staff-facing documents accessible:** headings, alt text on images, sufficient contrast, readable fonts, accessible PDFs, and plain language — this manual's quick-reference guides included.
7. **Website and public content:** meet ⬜ [WCAG 2.1 AA]; assess annually; remediate findings on a tracked plan; provide an accessible contact method and a stated process for requesting an alternative format.
8. **Communications with individuals served and families:** offer plain language, large print, translation, and alternative formats; never rely on a single channel that excludes people without internet access.
9. **Assistive and adaptive technology used by individuals served** is governed by program and clinical policy; IT supports the technical environment (connectivity, device management, security) and coordinates through the `Facilities – Accessibility/Adaptive Equipment` work order category.
10. **Prioritize** work orders affecting an accommodation or an individual's assistive technology at least one priority level higher than the equivalent routine request.
11. **Review** accessibility annually at the Technology Governance Committee: procurement compliance, open accommodations, website assessment, and complaints.

**J. Escalation.** Accommodation delayed beyond the target → HR + COO · An inaccessible system blocking an employee → IT + HR immediately; interim workaround required · Website accessibility complaint → Marketing + Legal + COO · Assistive technology failure affecting an individual served → P1/P2 per SOP-03 and program leadership.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Accommodation records (HR retention; medical information retained separately by HR) · procurement accessibility assessments (life of product + 7 years) · website assessments and remediation plans (7 years) · complaints (7 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Technical assessment of an accommodation request within 5 business days · Implementation within 10 business days of approval · Annual website assessment completed · Accessibility evaluated on 100% of new procurements.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Accessibility in the procurement checklist and actually applied ☐ Accommodation implementation time tracked ☐ Training materials available in accessible formats ☐ Website assessed within 12 months with a remediation plan ☐ No medical information held by IT ☐ Assistive technology work orders prioritized correctly.

**N. Related.** SOP-13 · SOP-20 · SOP-23 · HR accommodation policy · Employee Handbook

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.

# SOP-25 — BUSINESS CONTINUITY FOR LOSS OF POWER, PHONE, INTERNET, CLOUD, OR CRITICAL VENDOR SYSTEMS

**B.** ITG-SOP-25 | **Version:** 1.0 | **Effective:** ⬜ [DATE]

**C. Purpose.** Keep residential and day program services operating safely when technology is unavailable. This SOP is about keeping services running; SOP-15 is about getting systems back.

**D. Scope.** All sites and functions during loss of: electrical power, telephone service, internet, a cloud platform, or a critical vendor system (EHR, billing, payroll, alarm monitoring).

**E. Owner.** Accountable: [Chief Operating Officer] · Responsible: [VP of Operations] (program continuity) and [Director of Information Technology] (technical) · Consulted: Clinical, Nursing, Finance, HR, Compliance, Facilities

> **Authority note.** Program continuity execution sits with Operations, Clinical, and Nursing — two of which are peer functions. Per Section 6.2, the downtime procedures referenced here must be authored or co-authored by those functions. IT provides the trigger, the tools, and the communication; it does not write clinical downtime procedure.

**F. Required systems/tools/forms.** Site roster (Section 2.3) · paper downtime packets ⬜ **[BUILD REQUIRED — per site, per function]** · mass-notification channel ⬜ [INSERT] · hotspot inventory · generator and UPS inventory ⬜ [INSERT] · emergency contact lists (printed) · Appendix M

**G. Definitions.** *Downtime procedure* — the documented paper-based process used when a system is unavailable. *Recovery documentation* — entering downtime records into the system after restoration. *Critical function* — one that cannot be suspended without risk to safety, compliance, or revenue.

**H. Roles.** Site supervisor: activate downtime procedures, account for staff and individuals served, communicate · VP of Operations: cross-site decisions, staffing · Clinical/Nursing: clinical downtime documentation and medication procedures · Finance: payroll and billing continuity · IT: technical restoration and communication · COO: declaration, escalation, emergency spend.

**I. Procedure.**

**I.1 — Loss of electrical power**
1. **Ensure safety first**: account for all individuals served and staff; check on anyone dependent on powered medical or assistive equipment **immediately**; follow the site's emergency plan.
2. **Confirm** whether a generator started and is carrying the intended load; **if** not, **then** notify Facilities immediately.
3. **Notify** the on-call manager, Facilities, and the utility; record the outage start time.
4. **Assess** heating/cooling, refrigeration (food and medication storage — record temperatures), water (well pumps), elevators and lifts, door access, fire alarm, and emergency call systems.
5. **If** temperature, medical equipment, or life safety cannot be maintained, **then** initiate the site's relocation/evacuation plan per program policy; this is an Operations and Clinical decision, not a technology decision.
6. **Understand** that VoIP phones, Wi-Fi, and network equipment fail on power loss even where laptops still run on battery. Use cell phones; confirm the site's designated backup calling method.
7. **Shut down** equipment safely if the outage will be extended and UPS capacity is limited; IT directs.
8. **After restoration:** verify systems came back (network, phones, printers, time clocks, door access, alarms); reset anything that did not; log the outage; complete recovery documentation.

**I.2 — Loss of telephone service** → SOP-05 I.6. Confirm the backup calling method, confirm 911 reachability, notify on-call management, log start and end times.

**I.3 — Loss of internet** → SOP-06. Switch to paper downtime forms, deploy a hotspot per SOP-06 I.7, prioritize emergency communication and clinical documentation.

**I.4 — Loss of a cloud platform (Google Workspace, EHR, billing, payroll)**
1. **Confirm** the scope: our access only, or the vendor's service overall (check the vendor's status page).
2. **Notify** IT; IT opens a vendor ticket, records the reference number, and communicates the expected restoration.
3. **Switch** to the documented downtime procedure for that system:

| System unavailable | Continuity action | Owner |
|---|---|---|
| Email | Use phone and the mass-notification channel; do not use personal email for organizational business | All |
| Clinical/EHR documentation | Paper downtime forms; document times; enter within ⬜ [INSERT] hours of restoration | Clinical/Nursing |
| Medication documentation | Paper MAR per clinical policy — **procedure owned by Nursing** | Nursing |
| Billing | Hold and batch; confirm submission deadlines and extension processes | Billing/Finance |
| Payroll / timekeeping | Paper time records; contact the payroll vendor about the deadline; use prior-period estimates only with Finance approval | HR/Finance |
| Work order system | Phone intake; paper log (Appendix A printed); enter when restored | IT/Facilities |
| Incident reporting (UIR) | Paper UIR; submit within required timelines regardless of system status | Program/Compliance |
| Shared files | Use locally cached copies read-only; do not create parallel versions that will conflict on restoration | All |

4. **Communicate** on a cadence per SOP-17.
5. **On restoration:** verify functionality, complete recovery documentation, and reconcile any duplicate or conflicting entries.

**I.5 — Loss of a critical vendor system.** Same as I.4, plus: confirm the vendor's incident communication, assess whether our data is affected (treat as a security incident if so — SOP-08), and escalate contractually per SOP-16.

**I.6 — Preparation (the part that actually determines the outcome)**
1. **Maintain at every site:** printed emergency contact lists; printed downtime forms for every critical function; printed site roster and schedule; a charged designated backup phone; flashlights and batteries; printed route sheets; and a printed copy of the relevant quick-reference guides from Part V.
2. **Verify** these quarterly as part of site checks. ⬜ **[Assign this verification explicitly — an unverified downtime packet is reliably out of date by the time it is needed.]**
3. **Test** the mass-notification channel ⬜ [recommend quarterly].
4. **Drill** ⬜ [recommend semi-annually, rotating sites]: simulate a full site outage, run the procedures, and record what did not work.
5. **Review** generator and UPS coverage, testing, and fuel arrangements with Facilities annually.
6. **Document** every site's backup calling method, backup connectivity option, and generator status in the site roster.

**J. Escalation.** Any threat to resident safety → 911 and program emergency procedures immediately · Site cannot maintain temperature, power to medical equipment, or emergency communication → VP of Operations + COO immediately; relocation assessment · Outage > 4 hours at a residential site → COO + CEO · Clinical documentation unavailable beyond ⬜ [INSERT] hours → Chief Clinical Officer + Compliance · Payroll at risk → Director of Finance + COO immediately.

**K. Documentation/retention.** Outage and continuity logs (7 years) · downtime documentation (per clinical/program retention) · recovery documentation reconciliation (7 years) · drill records (7 years) · quarterly site verification records (3 years).

**L. Service-level targets** *(proposed).* Downtime procedures activated within 15 minutes of an outage being confirmed · Backup calling method confirmed within 15 minutes at residential sites · Hotspot deployed within 2 hours where needed · Recovery documentation completed within ⬜ [INSERT] hours of restoration · Quarterly site verification at 100%.

**M. QC checklist.** ☐ Downtime packets present and current at every site ☐ Backup calling method documented and tested per site ☐ Mass-notification channel tested quarterly ☐ Generator/UPS coverage documented ☐ Semi-annual drill completed ☐ Recovery documentation completed after every event ☐ Clinical downtime procedures authored by Clinical/Nursing.

**N. Related.** SOP-05 · SOP-06 · SOP-15 · SOP-17 · Appendix M · Emergency Preparedness Plan

**O. Revision notes.** 1.0 ⬜ Initial issue.
